## 1. [Searching for Dense Perturbers in Cold Dark Matter Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27236) <a id="link1"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27236

**Authors:** Xingyu Zhang, Hai-Bo Yu, Demao Kong, Ethan O. Nadler

**Abstract:** Recently, several low-mass, dense perturbers have been inferred from astrophysical observations of gravitational lensing, stellar streams, and satellite galaxies. If interpreted as dark matter subhalos, their inner densities exceed the predictions of the standard cold dark matter (CDM) model. We systematically investigate this tension using a CDM-only zoom-in simulation of a Milky Way analog. We analyze more than 5000 subhalos with masses down to $10^6\,M_\odot$ and reconstruct their inner density profiles using a tidal truncation model. The simulated subhalos are then compared with the observed compact perturbers in terms of their density profiles, enclosed masses, concentrations, and projected cylindrical masses. None of the simulated CDM subhalos is sufficiently dense to reproduce the inferred mass distributions of the observed perturbers. We further examine the redshift evolution of subhalo concentrations and derive a fitting function to characterize this evolution. Our findings motivate further investigations into exceptionally rare formation histories that could produce such dense perturbers, as well as the possibility of physics beyond the standard CDM model.

**Comment:** MW subhalo density profiles and compact perturbers in simulations

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 2. [Large fluctuations and cusp-core transformation in dwarf galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28583) <a id="link2"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28583

**Authors:** Mahmoud Hashim, Amr El-Zant, Francoise Combes

**Abstract:** Dwarf galaxies are abundant and dark matter dominated, rendering them particularly relevant for testing a cosmological model dominated by a dark sector. Whether their density profiles agree with $\Lambda$CDM expectations has long been a point of contention, dubbed the cusp/core problem. While numerical simulations have suggested that the erasure of central dark matter cusps may arise from potential fluctuations in feedback-driven gas, whether the required fluctuation levels are achieved in actual galaxies has remained uncertain. By measuring the power spectra of neutral gas density contrasts in a sample of gas-rich dwarf galaxies, we show that the observed fluctuations match theoretical expectations when their impact is derived from a stochastic dynamical model. Specifically, the relaxation times for core formation at the observationally estimated radii are generally on the order of a Hubble time or less. Core sizes are therefore consistent with conservative estimates assuming their generation through steady fluctuations acting over cosmic history. The relaxation time increases with halo mass, suggesting that core formation via this mechanism becomes less efficient in more massive systems.

**Comment:** Cusp-core transformation from gas fluctuations in dwarf galaxies

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 3. [A $\wedge$-like metallicity profile in the disk of the Sagittarius dwarf galaxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27526) <a id="link3"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27526

**Authors:** Zixi Guo, Xiang-Xiang Xue, Jiang Chang, Chao Liu, Zehao Zhong, Bingqing Zhang, Ruizhi Zhang, Shi Shao, Gang Zhao, Zhuohan Li, Xi Kang, Jie Wang, Jianhui Lian, Haining Li, Ruizheng Jiang

**Abstract:** Unveiling the properties of the progenitors that formed the tidal structures of the Milky Way (MW) is essential for exploring the MW's assembly history. Such studies further afford a unique opportunity to dissect the formation and evolution of dwarf galaxies by contrasting surviving and disrupted systems. However, reconstructing the intrinsic structure of progenitors solely from the positions and velocities of their tidal debris remains a formidable challenge. Here, we decode the chemo-kinematic signatures embedded in the stellar streams of the Sagittarius (Sgr) dwarf galaxy through N-body simulations. Our simulation reproduces a vertical metallicity gradient in the Sgr streams. We show that this feature is naturally explained if the stream originated from a disk component with a $\wedge$-like metallicity profile. Remarkably, similar metallicity profiles are identified in MaNGA survey data for a few (3/217) field dwarf galaxies, suggesting this feature is rare but not unique to Sgr. The vertical metallicity gradient of stellar streams preserves extra detailed galactic fossil records, enabling quantitative reconstruction of the peculiar structure of the satellite galaxy. The similar metallicity profiles of Sgr and MW raise the question of a possible special coevolution.

**Comment:** Sagittarius dwarf galaxy streams and progenitor structure

**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 4. [The chemical evolution of the LMC from APOGEE abundances: a hybrid two-infall OMEGA+ analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27831) <a id="link4"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27831

**Authors:** Erik Gasteiger, Viola Hegedűs, Szabolcs Mészáros

**Abstract:** The Large Magellanic Cloud (LMC) provides resolved stellar abundances and star-formation histories that can be used together to test models of dwarf-galaxy chemical evolution. We investigated if the non-monotonic APOGEE $[\mathrm{Mg}/\mathrm{Fe}]$-$[\mathrm{Fe}/\mathrm{H}]$ sequence can be explained by a hybrid history that combines a second gas-infall episode with a temporary increase in star-formation efficiency (SFE). We selected 4933 LMC red-giant-branch stars from APOGEE-2 DR17. We fitted an adaptively binned median Mg sequence with the two-zone open-box code OMEGA+. The formal fitting objective contained only the Mg sequence. The metallicity distribution function (MDF), one-dimensional abundance distributions, star-formation history (SFH), present-day mass partition, SFR, and supernova (SN) rate were retained as posterior diagnostics. The best-fitting model reproduces the Mg sequence with an RMS residual of 0.01 dex and also gives a close posterior match to O. It contains two delayed-tau infall episodes, with the second beginning at $t_2=10.39$ Gyr and with integrated mass ratio $M_2/M_1=0.159$, followed by a temporary Gaussian SFE enhancement centred at $t=11.91$ Gyr. The second infall is represented by a mixture of enriched and primordial gas. The model reproduces the timing of the late SFH enhancement and yields a present SFR within the adopted comparison range. A hybrid second-infall and SFE-enhancement history is a plausible scenario for reproducing the observed LMC Mg median sequence. Our fit explains the LMC evolution fairly well, but the remaining small MDF, mass, and SN-rate discrepancies suggest that a selection-function-aware treatment of the stellar distributions is required for a stricter comparison.

**Comment:** Chemical evolution and star formation history of LMC dwarf satellite

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 5. [KMOS Spectroscopic Follow-up of 16 Star Cluster Candidates of the Sagittarius Dwarf Galaxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28374) <a id="link5"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28374

**Authors:** C. O. Obasi, J. G. Fernández-Trincado, D. Minniti, M. Gómez, E. R. Garro, R. K. Saito, J. Alonso-García, V. D. Ivanov, J. Corral-Santana, A. Luna, Z. Guo, R. Kurtev, J. Borissova, V. Fermiano, C. Morris, B. Dias, C. Caceres, S. Saroon, J. B. Pullen, P. I. A. Rodríguez Cuevas, M. Lad, S. Federle, I. Petralia, P. W. Lucas, D. Bhadrakumar Sindhu, P. Esteves, M. G. Navarro, T. Palma, L. D. Baravalle, E. O. Schmidt, D. Galdeano, M. V. Alonso, J. L. Nilo Castellón, A. -N. Chené, J. Osses

**Abstract:** The Sagittarius (Sgr) dwarf spheroidal galaxy hosts several known globular clusters and numerous additional cluster candidates identified toward its crowded central regions. Establishing the nature of these candidates is difficult because apparent spatial and astrometric structure can arise from the underlying Sgr population. We investigate sixteen Minni stellar-cluster candidates with KMOS spectroscopy to determine whether the observed stars are compatible with Sgr and whether any candidate field shows evidence for a distinct stellar system. We analyse H-band KMOS spectra of 341 targets from the VVVX-GalCen Spectroscopic Survey, deriving radial velocities (RVs) and empirical metallicities. We compare these with an independent Sgr spectroscopic sample and use VVVX photometry and Gaia DR3 astrometry to test for localized stellar excesses relative to local control fields. Of the 341 KMOS targets, 256 (75%) have RVs within the adopted Sgr interval, and their metallicities broadly overlap the Sgr comparison population. No field shows statistically compelling kinematic evidence for a distinct system after accounting for the multiple candidates examined. Minni 329 shows the strongest spatial excess, but this is not significant after multiple-testing correction and is not accompanied by significant central concentration. The KMOS targets predominantly trace the Sgr population, but Sgr compatibility alone does not confirm the individual candidates as distinct stellar clusters. None of the sixteen fields can be confirmed as a separate stellar system with the present data, although this does not establish that the original candidates are spurious. Deeper imaging and larger spectroscopic samples are required to determine their physical nature.

**Comment:** Spectroscopic study of star clusters in Sagittarius dwarf galaxy

**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 6. [Metal-poor molecular clouds in dwarf galaxies at parsec-scale resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27064) <a id="link6"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27064

**Authors:** L. K. Hunt, M. Heyer, U. Lisenfeld, A. Weiss, A. Bolatto, E. Brinks, M. Rubio, D. Calzetti, F. Combes, E. Corbelli, B. G. Elmegreen, S. Garcia-Burillo, C. Henkel, K. M. Menten, L. J. Smith, A. Adamo, B. T. Draine, D. A. Hunter, N. Izumi, K. E. Johnson

**Abstract:** Understanding how stars form at low metallicity is challenging because carbon monoxide (CO) emission, which usually probes the molecular gas reservoir, is faint, and tends to be concentrated in parsec-scale clumps. To overcome this difficulty, in order to study the molecular clouds in metal-poor environments, we have acquired CO(2-1) ALMA maps at 3-4 pc resolution of four nearby dwarf galaxies with nebular (O/H) metallicities ranging from roughly 1/9 Z_sun to 1/3 Z_sun. Significant CO signal in the cubes has been identified through two masks, a ``strict'', high-confidence, mask and a ``broad'' one for better completeness. The former traces the cores of the clouds, while the latter includes fainter emission in the vicinity of the cores. Through a cloud-finding algorithm applied to the two sets of masked cubes, we have identified more than 200 molecular clouds across the sample, with roughly 50 in NGC3109, at 1/9 Z_sun, the lowest metallicity parsec-scale cloud population yet observed. The best-fit size-linewidth relations for the resolved clouds are similar among all galaxies, with a slope like the Milky Way (MW), but with lower velocity dispersion at a given cloud size. For a fixed CO-to-H_2 MW conversion factor, the cloud virial ratios tend to be large, and show a weak increasing trend with increasing cloud size, and decreasing apparent surface densities and metallicity. We find evidence for faint but significant CO emission outside CO-bright cores, spanning similar velocities to those observed in the cores. We attribute this to effective shielding by gas and dust, despite the low metallicities of our sample, since the CO tends to be cospatial with dust as traced by far-infrared emission.

**Comment:** Parsec-scale molecular clouds in nearby metal-poor dwarf galaxies

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 7. [Understanding the formation of alpha-Fe bimodality in the Milky Way using constrained cosmological simulations of the Local Group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27985) <a id="link7"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27985

**Authors:** Anna Genina, Ewoud Wempe, Amina Helmi, Hanneke C. Woudenberg, Simon D. M. White, Robert J. J. Grand, Ruediger Pakmor, Guilhem Lavaux, Jens Jasche

**Abstract:** We use a novel suite of constrained cosmological simulations of the Local Group to investigate the formation of alpha--Fe bimodalities in stellar discs of Milky Way analogues. In agreement with past studies using the Auriga model, we show that in our simulations the high-alpha sequence forms in a merger-induced starburst, which further quenches star formation, allowing the new stellar populations to form at low [alpha/Fe]. Producing a bimodality that is prominent at large radii, with a significant [alpha/Fe] gap and overlapping metallicity as in the Milky Way, requires the quenching of star formation across all radii where the bimodality is observed. The quenching can be achieved either with a galaxy-wide starburst, or with a retrograde merger reducing angular momentum in the outer disc and allowing more efficient dilution by the metal-poor gas in the circumgalactic medium (CGM). We show that mergers can also tilt the disc, changing the direction of galactic outflows and enriching a larger volume of the CGM with galactic metals, leading to low [alpha/Fe] scatter in the subsequently formed low-alpha sequence. We argue that the slopes in [alpha/Fe] of the two alpha sequences are crucial in narrowing down the mechanism that leads to their formation. We additionally uncover an indirect role that mergers play in enhancing the accretion of metal-poor gas onto galaxies. The smoothly accreted gas can dominate the gas content in the CGM and aid in the formation of distinct chemical sequences, especially at the low-metallicity end.

**Comment:** Constrained Local Group simulations studying CGM and galactic outflows

**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 8. [Constraints on Dark Matter Annihilation from Starlight-Induced Inverse Compton Emission in Omega Centauri](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28068) <a id="link8"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28068

**Authors:** Guan-Sen Wang, Xu Pan, Lei Feng

**Abstract:** The search for the particle identity of dark matter (DM) remains one of the foremost challenges in modern physics. In this work, we derive new constraints on the DM annihilation cross section $\langle\sigma v\rangle$ by considering the inverse Compton (IC) scattering of DM annihilation injected $e^+e^-$ off the starlight radiation field in Omega Centauri. As a likely remnant core of a dwarf galaxy, Omega Centauri provides a dense stellar environment that enhances the predict IC signal. Using multi-wavelength observations from Chandra and Fermi-LAT, we establish upper limits on $\langle\sigma v\rangle$ over a wide DM mass range from MeV to TeV. Notably, in the tens-of-MeV range, our results surpass existing constraints by approximately two orders of magnitude, exceeding $\langle\sigma v\rangle \sim 10^{-31}~{\rm cm^3\,s^{-1}}$. At several GeV to the TeV DM masses range, our constraints also remain stronger than existing limits. Our results demonstrate that starlight induced IC emission is an important component for indirect DM searches in dense stellar systems, establishing Omega Centauri as a powerful probe of the nature of DM.

**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 9. [Inferring the shapes of low-mass galaxies at $z>6$ from FIREbox$^{HR}$. Where are the disks?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26969) <a id="link9"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26969

**Authors:** Haojie Geng, Robert Feldmann, Jorge Moreno, Xuejian Shen, James Bullock

**Abstract:** Context. The three-dimensional shapes of galaxies provide clues to their formation and evolution. Inferring intrinsic shapes from projected sizes and axis ratios requires assumptions about the population distribution of shape parameters.   Aims. We test commonly adopted assumptions in Bayesian shape inference for high-redshift galaxies.   Methods. We analyze FIREboxHR galaxies with $\log(M_\star/M_\odot)=6.5-9.5$ at $z\sim6-15$. We measure intrinsic shapes from stellar particles using the minimum covariance determinant (MCD) estimator. We also create mock JWST/NIRCam images, measure half-light radii and axis ratios, and infer intrinsic distributions from the joint distribution of projected axis ratio $q$ and size $\log a$.   Results. Low-mass galaxies are predominantly prolate, with a substantial spheroidal component. Intrinsic ellipticity $E$ and triaxiality $T$ are well described by Johnson SB distributions, while intrinsic size $A$ is approximately log-normal. We therefore model $(\mathrm{logit}(E),\mathrm{logit}(T),\log A)$ with a multivariate normal distribution and allow general correlations. This model recovers the particle-measured shape distributions more faithfully than a multivariate normal model in $(E,T,\log A)$. Triaxiality-related parameters remain difficult to constrain from projected data without large samples or informative priors.   Conclusions. Bayesian forward modeling can recover intrinsic shape distributions when its population model adequately describes the underlying galaxies. An unsuitable distribution can bias inferences about morphological evolution at high redshift.

**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 10. [ASTROLABE: A 3D Particle-Level Surrogate for Cosmology Simulation with Physical Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27068) <a id="link10"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27068

**Authors:** Ibrahim Elsharkawy, David Curtin, Yonatan Kahn, Neal Weiner

**Abstract:** The microphysics of dark matter (DM) is imprinted on cosmological structure across an enormous dynamic range. Nonlinear cosmological observables are most reliably modeled with N-body and hydrodynamic simulations. However, systematic N-body scans over DM theory space are prohibitively expensive. Machine learning emulators provide a possible solution, but are currently limited by either low resolution or only the ability to emulate specific observables. We introduce Astrolabe, a flow-matching-based generative emulator designed to be free of both constraints. Astrolabe acts directly on the 6N phase-space coordinates of N individual particles. With no voxel grid in the output, there is no voxel-resolution ceiling, and in principle any observable can be measured from inference. Astrolabe is computationally tractable via a field-particle hybrid transformer in which every particle cross-attends to multiscale field tokens, so compute and memory are linear in N. Astrolabe incorporates physical flow matching by taking the prior distribution for the flow to be approximate COmoving Lagrangian Acceleration (COLA)-based coordinates rather than unphysical noise, with momentum and mass conservation enforced at all points along flow time. In the Lambda-CDM cases we test, Astrolabe produces realistic fields with accurate power spectra, halo mass functions, velocity profiles, and halo density profiles. We also observe out-of-distribution generalization, with accurate emulation of dark-acoustic oscillation initial conditions. Astrolabe is also capable of being fine-tuned to simulate very large N and we demonstrate this by emulating a TNG50-3-dark simulation at a fraction of the cost.This method represents a first step toward a general and powerful cosmology simulation surrogate, which may eventually permit the inclusion of baryonic physics and a systematic exploration of richer dark sector interactions.

**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 11. [A MUSE Study of Stellar and Ionized-Gas Kinematics in Central Group Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27169) <a id="link11"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27169

**Authors:** V. Olivares, E. O'Sullivan, P. Temi, S. Suárez, K. Kolokythas, M. Gaspari, F. Ubertosi, M. Aravena, F. Combes, Y. Su, P. Salome, E. Treister, M. Gitti, F. Brighenti, M. -L. Gendron-Marsolais

**Abstract:** Understanding gas accretion and feedback in low-mass galaxy groups bridges the gap between isolated ellipticals and cluster-dominant galaxies. We present new Multi Unit Spectroscopic Explorer (MUSE) observations of 17 nearby brightest group galaxies (BGGs), revealing diverse origins for their gas reservoirs. Warm ionized gas, traced by Halpha and [N II], is detected in 77% (13/17) of the sample, with morphologies including rotating disks with or without tails and diffuse emission, rings, and filaments. Unlike brightest cluster galaxies, most BGGs host regularly rotating stars, whereas their gas displays diverse kinematics. Nearly half of the rotating gas disks are aligned with the stars, consistent with internal recycling. However, tails, diffuse emission, or rings in three aligned systems may retain signatures of past accretion or interactions. The remaining disks, ring-like structures, and most filamentary systems are misaligned, supporting gas acquisition through mergers, interactions, or halo cooling. Compact disks are consistent with centrally concentrated, low-angular-momentum gas, possibly linked to residual cooling. Most filamentary systems lie near the precipitation threshold (tcool/teddy ~ 1-2) and preferentially occupy the turbulence-dominated region of the k-plot, supporting condensation consistent with chaotic cold accretion. All galaxies with detected X-ray halos, including those without warm gas, exhibit low central entropies (<30 keV cm^2) and short central cooling times (<3 Gyr). These conditions alone cannot predict multiphase gas, suggesting that AGN- or interaction-driven perturbations and turbulence-induced thermal instabilities help regulate condensation. No single mechanism dominates: BGGs are transitional systems in which internal recycling, environmental accretion, and cooling-driven condensation coexist, linking isolated ellipticals to cluster-dominant galaxies.

**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 12. [Annihilation feedback on elastic scattering in self-interacting dark matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27056) <a id="link12"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27056

**Authors:** Kalliopi Petraki, Thomas Pignard

**Abstract:** Sizable dark-matter self-interactions, motivated by observations of galactic structure, often arise from the exchange of light force mediators. Light mediators can render scattering nonperturbative and make inelastic processes important. Unitarity, via the optical theorem, implies that elastic and inelastic interactions cannot be treated independently: inelasticity induces absorptive contributions to the two-particle self-energy kernel that determines the elastic amplitude, while the resulting incoming-state dynamics also modifies the inelastic amplitudes. We study the feedback of dark-matter annihilation on elastic scattering in the presence of a long-range force. Although annihilation is a short-distance process, it can be strongly enhanced by such a force. We find that its feedback becomes significant near Sommerfeld resonances, where it suppresses the low-velocity growth of the baseline elastic cross section. Near baseline elastic-scattering zeros, it may instead generate a nonzero elastic floor required by unitarity. A companion paper studies the feedback of radiative bound-state formation on elastic scattering, which can generate sizable self-interactions.

**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 13. [Self-interacting dark matter from radiative bound-state-formation feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27089) <a id="link13"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27089

**Authors:** Marcos M. Flores, Kalliopi Petraki, Thomas Pignard

**Abstract:** Observations of galactic structure motivate sizable dark-matter self-interactions at low velocities, naturally realized through light force mediators. Such mediators can also make inelastic processes very efficient. Unitarity then requires their absorptive contributions to the two-particle self-energy kernel to be resummed, which in turn feeds back into both the elastic and inelastic scattering amplitudes. We study the feedback of radiative bound-state formation on elastic scattering and its implications for self-interacting dark matter. We focus on capture processes through emission of a charged scalar; such transitions are particularly efficient because the scattering and bound states are governed by different effective potentials, a feature also shared by radiative capture through emission of a non-Abelian gauge boson. We show that their feedback can generate sizable elastic scattering relevant for halo dynamics, even when the baseline elastic amplitude vanishes. Notably, the generated elastic cross section can grow toward low velocities, resembling long-range scattering. A companion paper examines the feedback from annihilation processes.

**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 14. [How does a galaxy's quenching probability depend on its black hole's mass?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27061) <a id="link14"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27061

**Authors:** Antonio J. Porras-Valverde, John C. Forbes

**Abstract:** We develop a parametric toy model to explore how black hole mass scatter $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ and the dependence of the quenching probability per unit time on black hole mass, parameterized as a power law with index $q$, jointly control the faint-end slope $\alpha$ of the stellar mass function of quenched central galaxies. By varying $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ and $q$, we identify two regimes. At low scatter, $\sigma_{\rm BH} \lesssim 0.4$ dex, $\alpha$ is strongly sensitive to $q$. Gradual quenching, represented by low $q$, produces shallow slopes, while sharp thresholds, with high $q$, produce steep mass functions, making $\alpha$ a powerful diagnostic of AGN feedback prescriptions. At large scatter, $\sigma_{\rm BH} \gtrsim 0.6$ dex, models with vastly different $q$ converge to similar quenched mass functions, because the intrinsic scatter erases the imprint of the feedback implementation. Comparing to cosmological hydrodynamical simulations and semianalytic models, only models with $\sigma_{\rm BH} \gtrsim 0.4$ dex match the observed faint-end slope of $\alpha \approx 0.7$. {\sc SAGE}, {\sc Dark Sage}, and {\sc COLIBRE}, with $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ of 2.0, 0.6, and 0.4 dex, respectively, reproduce this slope, whereas {\sc IllustrisTNG} and {\sc EAGLE}, with $\sigma_{\rm BH} \lesssim 0.2$ dex, produce steeper slopes. Current observational estimates of $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ allow a wide range of $q \gtrsim 0.8$, so the observed slope alone cannot identify the quenching prescription. If $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ is small, simultaneous measurements of $\alpha$ and $\sigma_{\rm BH}$ can distinguish between competing AGN feedback models.

## 15. [Single-Parameter Exponential Model Outperforms Standard Dark Matter and Modified Newtonian Frameworks in Predicting Galactic Rotation Curves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27876) <a id="link15"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27876

**Authors:** Mohammed Guhdar

**Abstract:** The functional forms of galactic rotation curves provide a fundamental test for models of gravity and dark matter. In this work, we conduct a rigorous statistical comparison of five distinct models against the complete sample of 175 galaxies from the SPARC database. The models under consideration are: (1) a one-parameter empirical law, physically constrained by each galaxy's measured flat disc rotation velocity ($V_{\text{flat}}$); (2) a classical two-parameter Freeman disk; (3) a three-parameter Navarro-Frenk-White (NFW) dark matter halo plus baryons model; (4) a one-parameter Modified Newtonian Dynamics (MOND) model; and (5) a two-parameter Bosma/Dark Matter Disk model. We employ a Bayesian framework with Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) for parameter estimation, and use the Akaike Information Criterion (AICc) to assess the goodness-of-fit and statistical parsimony of the best-fit models. Our results reveal a decisive preference for the constrained one-parameter empirical model. It is selected as the best descriptor for a clear majority of the sample (60.6\%) and, critically, also achieves the best median goodness-of-fit, with a reduced chi-squared of $\chi^2_{\nu} = 0.41$. In contrast, the standard NFW framework is preferred in only 12.6\% of cases, while the MOND and Freeman disk models are selected as optimal for just 10.3\% and 9.1\%, respectively. The Bosma model, despite linking dynamics to the gas distribution, is preferred in only 7.4\% of cases. The pronounced statistical and descriptive success of this simple, physically-anchored law over more complex, established theories suggests that its functional form represents a fundamental and highly efficient organizing principle in the dynamics of disk galaxies.

## 16. [Ionized Gas Kinematics and Star-forming Clumps in Extremely Metal-poor Galaxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27201) <a id="link16"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27201

**Authors:** Jyoti Yadav (IAC), Casiana Muñoz Tuñón (IAC), Cecilia Farina, Alejandro Lumbreras-Calle, Jorge Sánchez Almeida, Bruce G. Elmegreen

**Abstract:** Extremely Metal Poor (XMP) galaxies are the least chemically evolved objects in the local Universe and provide important constraints on galaxy formation in low-metallicity environments. This work investigates the ionised gas kinematics and star formation properties of XMP galaxies to identify disturbed or multi-component gas motions and explore their connection with star formation. We analyse long-slit spectroscopic observations of nine XMP galaxies obtained with the Intermediate Dispersion Spectrograph on the 2.5m Isaac Newton Telescope. The study uses the H$\alpha$ emission line to trace ionised gas and emission-line ratios to derive gas-phase metallicities. The kinematic analysis reveals that some galaxies exhibit ordered rotation, with kinematic centres not always coincident with the luminous star-forming heads. Most systems show disturbed, asymmetric, or declining velocity profiles, while localized distortions near bright star-forming heads suggest that massive clumps influence the surrounding gas dynamics. We derive dynamical masses for the dominant clumps and investigate the mass--metallicity and size--metallicity relations. The clumps exhibit low metallicities consistent with the XMP regime and comparable to those observed in low-mass dwarf galaxies. Three galaxies show counter-rotating or kinematically decoupled ionised-gas components, indicating complex gas kinematics and possible external gas acquisition. Our sample is predominantly composed of isolated systems, with metal-poor star formation concentrated in compact head regions. These properties may reflect gas accretion through mergers, interactions, or the acquisition of metal-poor gas from the intergalactic medium and cosmic filaments. Internal processes such as outflows, gas mixing, recycling, and stochastic star formation may also contribute to the observed properties.

## 17. [Photometric variability of blue supergiants: Constraints on stellar masses and evolutionary stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28352) <a id="link17"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28352

**Authors:** A. Alberici Adam, L. S. Cidale, M. Kourniotis, W. Glatzel, J. P. Sánchez Arias, M. Ruíz Díaz, A. Christen

**Abstract:** Blue supergiants (BSGs) are crucial to understanding the evolution of massive stars. Although distinguishing between pre- and post-red supergiant (RSG) phases is challenging, stellar pulsations provide valuable constraints on their masses and evolutionary status. This study analyzes TESS light curves of six BSGs with high L/M to identify periodicities. We performed theoretical modeling of the radial unstable modes to characterize the strange modes. We aim to estimate asteroseismic masses, compare them with spectroscopic and evolutionary mass estimates, and assess the evolutionary stage of the stars. We employed the Lomb-Scargle periodogram and the weighted wavelet Z-transform to address quasiperiodic behavior and time-varying signals. A linear nonadiabatic stability analysis was performed to test for radial perturbations, generating eigenfrequencies that enable stellar mass estimation. In addition, we characterized the stochastic low-frequency variability (SLFV). We derive a first guess at the asteroseismic masses. We find that HD 74371 and HD 79186 are evolving blueward following the RSG phase, while the evolutionary stage of HD 115842 cannot be uniquely determined with the available mass estimates. We also find that HD 52382 and HD 99953 are likely in the post-RSG phase; however, their low masses are difficult to reconcile with the available evolutionary models. The extreme supergiant HD 80077 shows the largest mass discrepancy in the sample, suggesting a more complex evolutionary history. The SLFV properties do not provide a clear diagnostic of the evolutionary stage. These results highlight the potential of asteroseismology as a complementary tool for constraining the evolutionary status of BSGs. However, the mass discrepancies found for some objects indicate that additional observational and theoretical efforts are required.

Algera, Manuel Aravena, Karin Cescon, Elisabete da Cunha, Pratika Dayal, Andrea Ferrara, Rebecca Fisher, Yoshinobu Fudamoto, Kasper E. Heintz, Hanae Inami, Andrea Pallottini, Lucie E. **Abstract:** Luminosity functions (LF) are some of the most basic quantities providing insights into galaxy evolution across cosmic time and enabling a direct comparison between observations and simulations. Here, we present the first [CII]158$\mu$m LF at $z\sim7$ from a sizeable UV-selected ALMA sample. Our analysis makes use of 27 galaxies with significant [CII] emission line detections in the epoch of reionization from the REBELS (Reionization Era Bright Emission Line Survey) ALMA Large Program and its pilot programs. We used these line detections and similar from ALPINE to calibrate UV luminosity-$L_{\mathrm{[CII]}}$ and stellar mass-$L_{\mathrm{[CII]}}$ relations at $z\sim7$. Given the challenge in precisely defining a selection volume for the REBELS sample itself, we rely on published UV LF and stellar mass function (SMF) determinations at $z\sim7$ and three complementary approaches to derive the [CII] LF. The UV LF-based method provides a lower limit on the [CII] LF, due to its missing the most dust-obscured systems. Our SMF-based estimate yields a Schechter function with a characteristic luminosity of $L^*_{\mathrm{[CII]}}=10^{9.11^{+0.11}_{-0.27}} L_\odot$ and a steep faint-end slope. From $z\sim7$ to $z\sim5$, our inferred [CII] LF shows an evolution of +0.3 dex in normalisation and +0.1 dex in $L^*_{\mathrm{[CII]}}$. Our results indicate a general underprediction from models of the abundance of [CII] emitters at high-redshift and thus potentially the number densities of massive yet UV-faint systems implied by our SMF-based method. Additionally, they suggest that 63% of the star formation rate density occurs in massive [CII]-bright systems and that $\sim$60-90% of [CII] emitters at $z\sim7$ may be missed by bright UV selections. This highlights the importance of surveys focusing on especially massive, UV-faint sources at $z>6$.

**Abstract:** We present a technique using integral-field spectroscopy to kinematically isolate intracluster light (ICL) from the brightest cluster galaxy (BCG), enabling insights into their contrasting stellar populations and the inner dark matter halo profile. Applying this technique to MUSE data of Abell S1063, we find that both components are old (~9 Gyr), but the ICL is more metal-poor than the BCG, implying distinct progenitor pathways. Within 100 kpc, the ICL comprises 55 per cent of the BCG+ICL stellar mass - substantially more than inferred from photometric decompositions. The BCG and ICL possess distinct velocity distributions, which remain nearly constant with radius, with an ICL velocity dispersion more than twice that of the BCG, and a line-of-sight velocity offset of 86 +- 18 km/s. Two-dimensional S\'ersic fits yield an ICL centroid offset of 5.2 +- 1.5 kpc from the BCG. These phase-space offsets are indicative of the BCG oscillating in a broad potential minimum. Furthermore, the ICL surface-brightness profile matches that of strong-lensing estimates assuming a cored halo. Simulations indicate that the inner halo profile varies systematically for different dark matter models. Applied to larger samples, our method therefore offers a complementary route to testing the nature of dark matter, while simultaneously revealing the stellar origins of the ICL.

**Abstract:** We report the discovery of 10 stars in the Praesepe and Hyades clusters with unusually slow rotation that are likely not field contamination. Even though our search was general, all slow rotators are in the fully convective domain. We find that the fraction of slow rotators in the clusters is more than $5\sigma$ above the distribution expected from field contamination. They provide a potential link between the period distribution of the young open clusters and the field. These stars could be a distinct birth population, or they could be an indication that the onset of strong magnetic breaking is earlier than previously realized in this domain. Photometric variability and X-ray luminosity suggest the later but more data is needed to confirm. Searches for similar populations in younger clusters will help determine when slow rotators first emerge and constrain the timescale and physical mechanisms governing angular-momentum loss in fully convective stars. The discovery of these stars also highlights the importance of long-baseline photometric observations for detecting the long rotation periods of low-mass stars.

**Abstract:** Age is the one of the most difficult stellar properties to constrain. However, upcoming surveys such as PLATO are about to revolutionize our ability to measure FGK dwarf ages with asteroseismology. Here, we use The Cannon to construct a data-driven model for inferring asteroseismic-quality age measurements from large spectroscopic survey observations and demonstrate that forthcoming asteroseismology datasets from future surveys can be leveraged, together with our model, to obtain stellar age samples larger than can be obtained by those surveys alone. We train The Cannon on 323 FGK dwarf stars with asteroseismic age labels from NASA Kepler photometry and high-resolution spectra from the Apache Point Observatory Galactic Evolution Experiment (APOGEE). We apply our model to FGK dwarfs from the Kepler survey that exhibit solar-like oscillations and reproduce their reported asteroseismic-quality ages with an RMS scatter of 1.0 Gyr. We then apply our model to the sample of 3810 Kepler FGK dwarfs with APOGEE spectra to infer data-driven asteroseismic ages. We repeat this for the larger sample of 48,828 FGK dwarfs with APOGEE spectra. Our work demonstrates that data-driven methods can robustly reproduce asteroseismic ages for FGK dwarfs. These methods will be invaluable tools for extending asteroseimology measurements from upcoming surveys (e.g., PLATO) to much larger stellar samples.

## 22. [VVV CL001: A strong candidate for the most ancient star cluster in the Galaxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27969) <a id="link22"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27969

**Authors:** J. Olivares Carvajal, Á. Valenzuela Navarro, C. Salvo-Guajardo, C. Ordenes-Huanca, B. Acosta-Tripailao, C. Quezada, F. Gran, M. Zoccali, M. Dall'Ora, M. De Leo, S. Villanova, D. Geisler

**Abstract:** Finding the oldest globular cluster of the Galaxy is important to constrain a lower limit on the age of the Galaxy and the Universe. VVV CL001 ([l,b]= [5.26,0.77]) is perhaps the most metal-poor ([Fe/H]~-2.2 dex) globular cluster in the Galactic bulge and a promising candidate to be the oldest globular cluster in the entire Milky Way. Due to its recent discovery and high extinction, its physical parameters, especially the distance and age, remain highly uncertain, yet they are essential to define it as an ancient native stellar system of the Milky Way. We aim to refine the distance and age of VVV CL001 using deep and high-quality photometry, to study its role in the formation of the Milky Way, and to determine whether it is the oldest Galactic globular cluster. We used J and KS observations to construct the deepest CMD of VVV CL001 to date, based on FourStar/Baade PSF photometry combined with HST near-IR wide-filter PSF photometry. We selected cluster members through proper motion calculations and the HDBSCAN algorithm, and assigned membership probabilities via Markov-Chain Monte Carlo iterations in a 5-dimensional parameter space. Using r band data from DECam/Blanco, we optically identified horizontal branch members and applied an isochrone-fitting technique to determine the cluster distance, while members around the main-sequence turn-off yielded the age from different isochrone sets. Finally, we refined the cluster orbital parameters. We obtained a distance of d = 7.34 +- 0.46 kpc, the most precise to date. We derived an age of 13.4 +- 0.4 Gyr, indicating that VVV CL001 is one of the oldest globular clusters in the Galaxy. The orbit confirms that the cluster belongs to the Galactic bulge, which, together with its age and metallicity, could link it to the early proto-Milky Way assembly, extending the metal-poor end of the known in situ globular clusters.

**Abstract:** We investigate spatial variations in polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon (PAH) properties and photoelectric heating efficiency in 67 nearby luminous infrared galaxies from the Great Observatories All-sky LIRG Survey (GOALS). In starburst galaxies, the PAH 11.3/7.7 ratio rises out to about 5 kpc while 6.2/7.7 remains nearly constant, indicating predominantly large, neutral PAHs. Between 5 and 8 kpc, 11.3/7.7 continues to rise while inferred PAH sizes decrease. Beyond 8 kpc, 11.3/7.7 falls and 6.2/7.7 rises, indicating smaller, more ionized PAHs. These trends are consistent with PAH photodestruction near the central starburst. Strong active galactic nucleus (AGN) systems show more neutral PAHs and harder radiation fields in their nuclear and inner regions. Beyond about 5 kpc, PAHs become more ionized and approach the starburst locus as the radiation field weakens. Strong AGNs influence average PAH charge states at 6-14 kpc as effectively as moderate AGNs at 1-6 kpc. Photoelectric heating efficiency increases with radius in both starburst and AGN-hosting galaxies as the radiation field weakens. In starbursts, higher efficiency is associated with more neutral PAHs, while AGN-dominated systems show the opposite relation. These results provide quantitative, spatially resolved constraints on the extent of AGN effects on dust properties in a population of luminous infrared galaxies.

## 24. [Probing Minihalos in the Late Dark Ages through Velocity Acoustic Oscillations in the 21-cm Power Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27933) <a id="link24"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27933

**Authors:** Yan Dong, Bin Yue, Chen Zhang, Yuewei Wen, Yidong Xu, Ayodeji Ibitoye, Xuelei Chen

**Abstract:** In the Dark Ages, minihalos above the Jeans mass accrete gas from the intergalactic medium (IGM). The accreted gas would be heated to virial temperature and produces 21 cm signal in emission, while the majority of gas retained in the IGM is cold and has 21 cm signal in absorption. The 21 cm signal of the IGM generally dominates over that of minihalos, making it difficult to extract the contribution of minihalos from the detected 21 cm signal. However, in the late Dark Ages, in regions where the gas has not yet been influenced by the radiation from Pop III stars, the 21 cm signal from the IGM is weak because collisional coupling has become less efficient. More importantly, the relic supersonic dark matter-baryon relative streaming velocities suppress the gas accretion of smaller minihalos and encode their large-scale coherent features into the 21 cm signal, generating the velocity acoustic oscillation (VAO) features on the 21 cm power spectrum. This offers a promising avenue for detecting minihalos. We found that, at $z\sim 15$ ($\sim 20$), the first peak of the net VAO wiggle at $k=0.05$ Mpc$^{-1}$ is $\sim 1.4\times 10^{-3}$ ($\sim 3.1\times 10^{-4}$) mK$^2$. With a total of 24 lunar nights/8064 hr (60 lunar nights/20160 hr) of integration, the signal from $z\sim 15$ ($\sim 20$) is detectable for an array composed of 82944 (278784) dipoles, spread over $\sim 3.5$ ($\sim 6.3$) km. If the small-scale matter power spectrum is enhanced and minihalos are much more numerous than in the standard $\Lambda$CDM model, the signal is detectable for an even smaller scale array.

**Authors:** Adam Brček, Ryosuke Hirai, Ilya Mandel, Jack Yutong Chen

**Abstract:** Interactions and mass loss during the main-sequence (MS) phase of massive binary stars can significantly alter their subsequent evolution and the properties of the black holes (BHs) they ultimately produce. We use the rapid binary population synthesis code \textsc{COMPAS}, incorporating a new treatment of MS evolution that self-consistently tracks the convective core under arbitrary mass-loss or mass-gain histories. We explore the properties of black hole high-mass X-ray binaries (BH-HMXBs) and binary black holes (BBHs) formed under this new framework. We find that a more detailed treatment of MS evolution is necessary to reproduce the high BH masses observed in BH-HMXBs, which are otherwise absent under prescriptions that do not take the internal structure of MS stars into account. Matching the observed rates and orbital periods favors case A mass transfer that is more non-conservative than commonly assumed. We further find that mass transfer of helium-enriched material during case A mass transfer is common in massive binaries, with potential consequences for the structure of the accreting star. For the BBH population, the new MS framework alters the chirp-mass and mass-ratio distributions of detectable mergers, and case A mass transfer can make a substantial contribution to the BBHs formed through the stable mass transfer channel. These systems show a strong preference for near-equal mass ratios, broadly consistent with recent constraints from gravitational-wave observations.

Most clumpy galaxies host only one or two robustly detected off-centre clumps, while higher-multiplicity systems occur preferentially in massive, strongly star-forming galaxies at low to intermediate redshift. Comparison with major merger rates suggests that mergers could account for a substantial fraction of low-mass ($\log_{10}(M_\star/{\rm M_\odot})9$) require an additional in situ clump-formation channel. ## 27. [Core-Collapse Supernova Rates in the Dark Energy Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27328) <a id="link27"></a>

**Authors:** K. Boyne, O. Graur, M. Vincenzi, P. Wiseman, M. Grayling, M. Smith, C. Frohmaier, C. Lidman

**Abstract:** Over its five-year campaign (2013-2017), the Dark Energy Survey (DES) supernova (SN) survey recorded 31,636 light curves. Using a custom-built, two-stage random forest classifier, we identified 2,445 likely core-collapse SNe (CC SNe) within the survey rate analysis footprint. With this sample, we measure the volumetric CC SN rate from redshift z = 0 to 1.3 in 11 redshift bins, representing the most detailed single-survey coverage to date across nine billion years of lookback time. Over this interval, the CC SN rates are well fit by a power law R_CC(z) = R_0 (1 + z)^\alpha, with R_0 = 1.58 \pm 0.17 \times 10^{-4} \text{ yr}^{-1} \text{ Mpc}^{-3} and \alpha = 2.85 \pm 0.25. The rate turnover, expected at cosmic noon, is not yet apparent. Because CC SNe trace recent star formation, we compare our measurements with recent cosmic star-formation history (CSFH) models. Our measurements are ~70 per cent higher than CSFH models using a Salpeter initial mass function (IMF) to predict CC SN rates. Replacing the Salpeter IMF with a Baldry and Glazebrook IMF and a CC SN progenitor mass range of 8-40 M_\odot bridges the gap. Alternatively, CSFHs may miss a hitherto unidentified population of low-luminosity galaxies. We further find that the local ratio of stripped-envelope (SE) SNe to all CC SNe is 31 \pm 5 per cent, remaining constant from z = 0 to 1.3. Combined with an IMF analysis accounting for the "missing progenitor problem" of hydrogen-rich SNe, our results favour a mixture of single massive stars and binaries as SE SN progenitors, with the redshift-invariant ratio favouring binary channels.

**Authors:** Timo Kravtsov, Hanindyo Kuncarayakti, Joseph P. Anderson

**Abstract:** Characterisation of progenitor stars of supernovae (SNe) poses several challenges. Direct observations of progenitors are based on serendipitious archival observations and are only possible for nearby events. Indirect methods include inferring nickel mass from the light curves or oxygen masses from the SN's nebular phase, both of which are tools to constrain the progenitor's initial mass. Alternatively, environments of SNe have been used as a proxy for the progenitor's age and metallicity. The progenitor channel of type Ibn SNe is a hotly debated topic. The mass-loss and the formation of He-rich circumstellar medium requires strong envelope stripping of the progenitor star. For core-collapse supernovae (CCSNe), this requires strong stellar wind mass losses, i.e. a Wolf-Rayet (WR) star, or binary interaction with a companion star. Here, we analyse a sample of host galaxies of type Ibn SNe that have been observed with MUSE. We use H$\alpha$ emission to infer the transient's association to star formation and derive the local metallicity with commonly used strong line diagnostic. We find that the sample of SNe are highly associated with star formation in the host galaxy, typically situated within one of the brightest HII regions in the host galaxy. The NCR distribution of H$\alpha$ pixels in SN Ibn environments strongly resembles that of WR stars, and shows a stronger preference to SF compared to other core-collapse SN types. Metallicity measurements show typically sub-solar metallicity in the SN environments.

**Authors:** Agripino Sousa-Neto, Susana J. Landau, Jailson Alcaniz

**Abstract:** Recent analysis combining baryon acoustic oscillation measurements with cosmic microwave background and Type Ia supernova data show an apparent preference for a time-dependent dark-energy equation of state within $w_0w_a$ parameterizations. Here we test the robustness of this result against an independent cosmological consistency requirement, namely that the expansion history must provide sufficient time to accommodate the oldest observed stellar populations. Motivated by recent stellar and globular-cluster age determinations, we impose progressively stronger lower bounds, $t_0 \geq 13.6$, $13.75$, $13.8$, and $13.85$ Gyr, on the expansion histories allowed by DESI DR2+CMB+Pantheon+ data. We find a systematic displacement of the posterior in the $(w_0,w_a)$ plane toward the $\Lambda$CDM prediction, $(w_0,w_a)=(-1,0)$, as the minimum allowed age is increased. For $t_0 \geq 13.85$ Gyr, the Mahalanobis separation from $\Lambda$CDM decreases from $2.56\sigma$ to $1.72\sigma$, bringing the $\Lambda$CDM prediction inside the 95\% credible region. Our results show that the current evidence for dynamical dark energy is sensitive to independent cosmic-age information and that stellar chronometry provides a direct robustness test of the DESI-favored expansion histories. Requiring sufficiently old cosmologies can appreciably reduce the statistical preference for departures from the standard $\Lambda$CDM model.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 30. [Graph Neural Networks for Astrophysics and Cosmology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27051) <a id="link30"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27051

**Authors:** Christian Kragh Jespersen

**Abstract:** Physics seldom makes breakthroughs when we consider objects only in isolation. Instead we model and measure how objects sit among their neighbours, spatial, temporal, or otherwise: a galaxy in its environment, a halo in its history, a photon hit in a detector. Graphs are ideally suited for encoding exactly these relations, and Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) learn the geometric functions that respect them. This review presents the use of GNNs for astrophysics and cosmology, and argues that GNNs often suit physics and physical data better than any alternative: they fit the native geometry of the domain and the signal, enable the incorporation of the symmetries and prior knowledge we already trust and have toiled to build, and stay flexible enough to capture what we do not yet understand. GNNs' physically intuitive workings also render them more interpretable than other deep-learning architectures. We show how we can begin from general principles of the geometry of deep learning and build the graph and the graph neural network from the ground up, and how other architectures, like the convolutional network and the transformer, can be seen as special cases of one unified geometric blueprint. We survey applications across cosmology, galaxy formation, dynamics, and instrumentation; and we chart the frontier. From this survey, we find that only one in six articles outside instrumentation applies GNNs to data rather than just simulations. We also distil a set of best practices, including when not to reach for a graph. Throughout, we will prioritise physical intuition and foundational principles over mathematical machinery whenever possible.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 31. [An improvement to the spatial discretisation in the Astrophysical Weighted Particle Hydrodynamics method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28006) <a id="link31"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28006

**Authors:** Alex Massaro Achá (Instituto de Astrofísica de Canarias, Universidad de La Laguna), Isaac Alonso Asensio (Instituto de Astrofísica de Canarias, Universidad de La Laguna), Claudio Dalla Vecchia (Instituto de Astrofísica de Canarias, Universidad de La Laguna)

**Abstract:** The discretisation of space in the Astrophysical Weighted Particle Hydrodynamics method is attained through a partition-of-unity-based cubature rule. The node (particle) weights in such rules must in general be obtained using approximations, for which only one has been thus far proposed in the literature. In this work, new approximations for these weights are proposed, their properties are studied, and their impact on the accuracy of the cubature rule and the overall method is assessed. Approximations for node centroids are also proposed and studied, and their possible applications are laid out. Additionally, for schemes in which the kernel-function compact-support size is set to be constant, rather than adaptive, weight and centroid approximations are provided as well. Finally, for the cubature rule arising from the weight approximation previously proposed in the literature, an asymptotic expression for its mean relative error is derived, and its usefulness for understanding the finite-sample properties of the cubature rule is showcased with some simple examples.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 32. [Phakoseismology: Asteroseismology of gravitationally microlensed stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28005) <a id="link32"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28005

**Authors:** Marcus L. Marcussen, Mikkel N. Lund

**Abstract:** Asteroseismology relies on disk-integrated photometry or radial velocities, in which geometric cancellation suppresses modes of high spherical degree ($l\geq4$). Observations are thus limited to deeply penetrating, low-degree modes, leaving the outer envelope inaccessible. We investigate whether a high-magnification microlensing event can temporarily lift this limitation by differentially magnifying the surface of an oscillating source star. We modelled the visibility of solar-like oscillations during microlensing, weighting the surface by limb darkening and the local magnification. We computed multiplet visibilities and the power distribution among azimuthal components, and built a likelihood model for recovering the 3D spin-axis orientation from resolved rotational multiplets. When the lens-source separation becomes comparable to the source's angular radius, the magnification gradient acts as a transient spatial filter that breaks the symmetry of disk integration, raising high-degree mode visibilities by up to three orders of magnitude. The same non-axisymmetric weighting makes the relative power of azimuthal components depend on both the inclination and sky-projected azimuth of the spin axis, imprinting the full spin orientation onto the power spectrum. With $5\%$ relative-power uncertainties, we recover the orientation to $\sigma_i=2.9^\circ$ and $\sigma_\gamma=11.5^\circ$. Microlensing thus does more than amplify distant stars: its magnification pattern enables spatially selective asteroseismology. The effect is strongest for rare close encounters and unlikely to support large samples, and the newly visible high-degree modes will overlap with rotationally split and mixed modes, complicating mode identification. Nevertheless, individual high-quality events may open a window on envelope physics and spin geometries for stars in the Galactic bulge and beyond.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 7

## 33. [The "Rubin 2036: Opportunities for Discovery" Workshop: A Community Roadmap for the Future of the Vera C. Rubin Observatory After the LSST](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28482) <a id="link33"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28482

**Authors:** Andrés Alejandro Plazas Malagón, Steve Allen, Shreya Anand, Muhammad Awais, Simon Birrer, Lindsey Bleem, Robert Blum, Adam S. Bolton, Rosaria Bonito, Alex Broughton, Jeffrey L. Carlin, Francisco Castander, Rebecca C. Chen, Anirudh Chiti, Lyla Choi, Chuck Claver, Suber Corley, John Franklin Crenshaw, Arjun Dey, Seth Digel, Holger Drass, Alex Drlica-Wagner, Haruki Ebina, Lukas Eisert, Anthony Englert, Kevin Fanning, Josh Frieman, Shenming Fu, Nandini Hazra, Carrie E. Holt, Minhee Hyun, Hafiz Inam Ullah, Saurabh Jha, J. Bryce Kalmbach, Yijung Kang, Kshitija Kelkar, Sthabile Kolwa, Andjelka Kovačević, Kenneth W. Lin, Meng-Xiang Lin, Becky Long, Sean MacBride, Alex I. Malz, Steve Margheim, Nigel J. Mason, Guillem Megias Homar, Simona Mei, Hironao Miyatake, Maia Moore, Justin Myles, Jeffrey A. Newman, Anh Nguyen, Yasna Ordenes-Briceño, Antonella Palmese, Swayamtrupta Panda, Claire Poppett, Francisco Pozo Nuñez, Anand Raichoor, Andrew Rasmussen, Maya Redden, Steve Ritz, Aaron Roodman, Leonid Sajkov, Ben Sherwin, Brian Stalder, Chris Stubbs, John Suárez-Pérez, Sandrine Thomas, Chun-Hao To, Alessandro S. Tramuto, Yousuke Utsumi, Laura Venuti, Hai-Feng Wang, Risa Wechsler, Christina C. Williams, W. Michael Wood-Vasey, Prince Yadav, Masaya Yamamoto

**Abstract:** The NSF-DOE Vera C. Rubin Observatory will complete its ten-year Legacy Survey of Space and Time (LSST) in the mid-2030s. Its 8.4 m aperture and 9.6 square degree field of view give it an \'etendue unmatched by any facility planned for that era. The Rubin 2036: Opportunities for Discovery workshop (KIPAC/SLAC, 3-5 August 2026) brought together approximately 100 participants to discuss Rubin's future after LSST. This paper summarizes the workshop's structure and discussions across five working groups. The continued-surveying group explored a machine-learning foundation model pretrained on Rubin imaging for detection, deblending, and classification. The cadence group considered allocating post-LSST observing time and strategies for transiting exoplanets, Type Ia supernova cosmology, time-delay cosmography, and second-scale transients. The filter group explored broadband, medium-band, and narrowband filters for photometric redshifts, high-redshift large-scale structure, AGN reverberation mapping, Galactic archaeology, and emission-line imaging. The multi-messenger group explored follow-up of next-generation gravitational-wave and neutrino sources, including standard sirens, kilonovae, and Galactic supernovae. The technology group organized options by cost and lead time, including fast readout, new filters, a faster shutter, and summit edge computing. Cross-cutting topics included AI/ML, Chilean and Latin-American co-leadership, satellite megaconstellations, and weather and climate trends at Cerro Pach\'on. White papers and workshop talks are available through https://zenodo.org/communities/rubin2036workshop/ and https://rubin-observatory.github.io/Rubin-Beyond-LSST/pages/Rubin2036proceedings/.

**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 34. [DDELenS: Tomographic Weak Lensing Simulations with Dynamical Dark Energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27088) <a id="link34"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27088

**Authors:** Shu-Fan Chen, J. Colin Hill, Zoltán Haiman

**Abstract:** We present a suite of 128 cosmological simulations in $w_0w_a$CDM cosmologies with massive neutrinos, designed to enable simulation-based inference of dynamical dark energy parameters from weak gravitational lensing and other cosmological observables. Each simulation follows $1024^3$ dark matter particles in a $512\,h^{-1}$Mpc periodic box, with cosmological parameters sampled using a Sobol sequence over the ranges $\Omega_m \in [0.28, 0.35]$, $\sigma_8 \in [0.74, 0.90]$, $w_0 \in [-1.0, -0.5]$, and $w_a \in [-1.5, 0.0]$, motivated by recent results from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument survey. The nonlinear evolution is computed with $\texttt{PKDGRAV3}$ using the N-body gauge to consistently incorporate relativistic corrections from massive neutrinos, photons, and dark energy. For each cosmology, we generate 10,000 ray-traced convergence maps at six source redshifts $z_s \in \{0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 1100\}$ using $\texttt{LensTools}$, and additionally provide halo catalogues and projected halo overdensity fields for galaxy--lensing cross-correlation analyses. We generate 100,000 realizations at the fiducial cosmology for the construction of the covariance matrix. This simulation suite provides a foundation for field-level inference and non-Gaussian statistics with next-generation weak lensing surveys such as the Rubin Observatory LSST, Euclid, and the Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope, as well as CMB lensing experiments including the Simons Observatory.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 35. [Leviathan: a very massive, extended sub-millimetre galaxy spectroscopically confirmed at $\rm z = 4.05$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27101) <a id="link35"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27101

**Authors:** William M. Baker, Francesco Valentino, Jens Hjorth, Gabriel Brammer, Ugnė Dudzevičiūtė, Kei Ito, Antonio Pensabene, Pengpei Zhu

**Abstract:** We present Leviathan, a sub-millimetre galaxy (SMG) spectroscopically confirmed at $z=4.051$ with JWST/NIRSpec. Modelling 31 bands of photometry from the rest-frame ultraviolet to the millimetre, we find a stellar mass of $\log(M_\star/M_\odot)=11.34^{+0.15}_{-0.22}$, making Leviathan one of the most massive spectroscopically confirmed galaxies at $z>4$, yet consistent with $\Lambda$CDM for a star-formation efficiency of approximately 20%. Although SMGs are often thought to be building the dense cores of compact quiescent galaxies, Leviathan is extended, with a rest-frame $0.9\,\mu\mathrm{m}$ half-light radius of 4.3 kpc. Spatially resolved SED fitting gives a half-mass radius of $3.1\pm0.4$ kpc, consistent with its size in rest-frame $1.5\,\mu\mathrm{m}$ imaging. This is three times larger than quiescent galaxies of the same mass, but consistent with the size-mass relation of star-forming galaxies at $4<5$ extrapolated to $M_\star=10^{11.3}M_\odot$. Leviathan forms stars on the main sequence (approximately $400\,M_\odot\,\mathrm{yr}^{-1}$) with dust spread across its disc. Both its stellar mass and dust are more extended than those of $z\approx3$ SMGs. Alongside GN20.2b, which has a similar mass and star-formation rate but is three times smaller, these show that massive dusty galaxies at $z\approx4$ can have very different stellar structures, and that not all have their stellar mass concentrated in a compact core.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 36. [Resolving the extended interstellar medium of infrared galaxies II. Fine-structure line emission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28597) <a id="link36"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28597

**Authors:** C. M. Lofaro, T. Diaz-Santos, L. Armus, V. Charmandaris, T. Lai, M. Pereira-Santaella, J. McKinney, R. Fernandez-Aranda, G. Donnelly, M. Bianchin, Y. Song, I. Shivaei, L. A. Boogaard, G. Rodighiero, S. Linden, G. C. Privon, C. Ricci, L. Lenkic, H. Inami, S. Stierwalt, V. U, D. Kakkad, P. Van der Werf, M. Sanchez-Garcia, A. Evans, D. B. Sanders

**Abstract:** We investigate how star formation and active galactic nuclei (AGNs) shape the ionized and neutral interstellar medium in 67 nearby luminous and ultraluminous infrared galaxies from the Great Observatories All-sky LIRG Survey (GOALS). Combining mid-infrared (MIR) spectral mapping from the Spitzer Infrared Spectrograph with far-infrared (FIR) spectroscopy from the Herschel Photodetector Array Camera and Spectrometer, we measure fine-structure emission lines in aperture grids extending to projected galactocentric distances of about 14 kpc. We compare the measurements with CLOUDY photoionization models to constrain the ionization parameter, hydrogen density, stellar age, and metallicity. In starburst-dominated galaxies, the combined MIR+FIR fits yield a decreasing ionization parameter with radius, approximately constant hydrogen densities, uniformly young stellar populations, and flat metallicity profiles. MIR-only fits instead yield an increasing ionization parameter with radius, highlighting the importance of FIR diagnostics, particularly the [C II] emission associated with photodissociation regions, in constraining the multiphase gas. In the strongest AGN-dominated system, the nucleus and inner extended regions show elevated [Ne III]/[Ne II] and suppressed [C II]/[O I] ratios, while the outermost regions, at projected distances of about 6 kpc and beyond, show values typical of star-forming regions. This suggests that the AGN may affect gas excitation out to about 5 kpc. Our results demonstrate the importance of combining MIR and FIR diagnostics to recover radial trends that can be hidden in galaxy-integrated measurements. Together with Paper I, they provide a benchmark for spatially resolved studies of gas and dust in starbursts and AGN-hosting galaxies.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

Uhlemann (Fakultät für Physik, Universität Bielefeld, Postfach 100131, 33501 Bielefeld, Germany, School of Mathematics, Statistics and Physics, Newcastle University, Herschel Building, Newcastle-upon-Tyne, NE1 7RU, UK), A. Venhola (Space physics and astronomy research unit, University of Oulu, Pentti Kaiteran katu 1, FI-90014 Oulu, Finland), D. Vergani (INAF-Osservatorio di Astrofisica e Scienza dello Spazio di Bologna, Via Piero Gobetti 93/3, 40129 Bologna, Italy), S. Vinciguerra (Aix-Marseille Université, CNRS, CNES, LAM, Marseille, France), M. von Wietersheim-Kramsta (Department of Physics, Institute for Computational Cosmology, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK, Department of Physics, Centre for Extragalactic Astronomy, Durham University, South Road, Durham, DH1 3LE, UK), L. Wang (SRON Netherlands Institute for Space Research, Landleven 12, 9747 AD, Groningen, The Netherlands, Kapteyn Astronomical Institute, University of Groningen, PO Box 800, 9700 AV Groningen, The Netherlands), A. H. Wright (Ruhr University Bochum, Faculty of Physics and Astronomy, Astronomical Institute), H. W. Yeung (Institute for Astronomy, University of Edinburgh, Royal Observatory, Blackford Hill, Edinburgh EH9 3HJ, UK), D. Scott (Department of Physics and Astronomy, University of British Columbia, Vancouver, BC V6T 1Z1, Canada)

**Abstract:** We present a simulation framework for Euclid galaxy clustering measurements that reproduces the essential behaviour of the spectroscopic reduction chain, bypassing the computation required by full pixel-level simulations. The framework comprises two complementary tools, FastSpec, which simulates 1-dimensional slitless spectra with a simplified instrument and noise model, and Pypelid, which computes detection probabilities on input catalogues, accounting for the survey dithering sequence and spatially varying noise. We develop calibrated prescriptions for the survey selection function, redshift errors, and redshift interloper populations. Applied to the Euclid Wide Survey, the baseline selection yields a redshift purity of 74% and a sample completeness of 45%, which improves with the use of Euclid photometric colours. The redshift error distribution is found to have a Gaussian core of width $\sigma_z = 9.4 \times 10^{-4}$, with no significant trend over $0.9 < z < 1.8$. Emission lines [OIII]5008 and [SIII]9530 misidentified as H$\alpha$ contribute a few to ten per cent depending on redshift, while catastrophic redshift measurement failures dominate at low signal-to-noise ratio. Simulating the Data Release 1 footprint, we find that complex survey selection effects can be corrected with a forward-modelled random catalogue, recovering the monopole and quadrupole of the galaxy 2-point correlation function. The simplified noise model does not include residual contamination from overlapping spectra, or other artefacts seen in early Euclid data; this will be revisited with DR1 data. The framework provides an efficient route for characterising the Euclid spectroscopic selection function at the precision required for galaxy clustering science, and is the basis for systematic error assessments in Euclid galaxy clustering analyses.

**Novelty:** 6

## 38. [SN 2023iop: An Extreme 1987A-like Supernova and the Death of Very Massive Stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27312) <a id="link38"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27312

**Authors:** Steve Schulze, Avinash Singh, Nikhil Sarin, Ofer Yaron, Jesper Sollerman, Takashi J. Moriya, Avishay Gal-Yam, Bart van Baal, Stan Barmentloo, Conor M. B. Omand, Hannah Wichern, Tuomas Kangas, Priscila J. Pessi, Adam A. Miller, Armin Rest, Lin Yan, Alexei V. Filippenko, Chang Liu, Miika Pursiainen, Yi Yang, Georgios Dimitriadis, Kishalay De, Seán J. Brennan, Thomas G. Brink, Ping Chen, Aleksandar Cikota, Felice Cusano, Nicholas Earley, Claes Fransson, Lluís Galbany, Anamaria Gkini, Anders Jerkstrand, Ragnhild Lunnan, Keiichi Maeda, Diego Paris, Andrea Rossi, Nabeel Rehemtulla, Claudia Skoglund, WeiKang Zheng, Alaa Alburai, Ke-Jung Chen, K-Ryan Hinds, Anjasha Gangopadhyay, Jonay Isaí Gonzalez-Hernandez, Assaf Horesh, Giorgos Leloudas, Eliana Palazzi, Daniel A. Perley, Frédérick Poidevin, Ramon Sanfeliu, Jacob L. Wise, Erez A. Zimmerman, Alice Cai, Joahan Jaimes, Ting-Wan Chen, Michael W. Coughlin, Richard Dekany, Ori D. Fox, Christoffer Fremling, Matthew J. Graham, Steven Groom, Mansi M. Kasliwal, Marek Kowalski, Russ E. Laher, Paraskevas Lampropoulos, Elisabetta Maiorano, Ester Marini, Jakob Nordin, Josiah Purdum, Reed Riddle, Niharika Sravan, Aswin Suresh

**Abstract:** Supernova (SN) 1987A showed that some hydrogen-rich core-collapse supernovae (SNe) rise slowly to peak, defining a distinct subclass. Wide-field surveys have uncovered more extreme members, such as OGLE-2014-SN-73, SN 2020faa, and SN 2023vbw, that challenge neutrino-driven explosion models. We present ultraviolet-to-infrared, radio, X-ray, and spectropolarimetric observations of SN 2023iop at $z = 0.1020$, the most extreme 87A-like event to date. It rose for $\gtrsim88$ rest-frame days to $M_r \approx -20.1$ mag, also placing it among the hydrogen-rich superluminous SNe, and radiated $\gtrsim4\times10^{50}$ erg. Its spectra resemble those of typical Type II SNe and remain photospheric at $\sim330$ days post-maximum. Continuum polarization of 0.8-1.0% indicates an aspherical system. Hydrogen-rich pair-instability supernova (PISN) models of a $190~M_\odot$ star reproduce the light-curve up to 150 days after peak, but not the late-time emission. A late-time spectrum shows a feature near 7300 {\AA}, tentatively identified as [Ni II] $\lambda$7378. The implied $\approx0.02~M_\odot$ of stable $^{58}$Ni is reached only in the most massive PISN models. The low-mass host galaxy ($\sim10^{8.2}~M_\odot$) is consistent with a metal-poor progenitor as required by the PISN channel. Fallback accretion reproduces the light-curve morphology, but not the colors. We consider a pair-instability explosion the most plausible scenario, with the late-time light-curve excess powered by circumstellar interaction, which shows no signatures in the spectra. SN 2023iop shows that the most luminous 87A-like events occupy an underexplored region of massive-star evolution.

## 39. [Globular Clusters GMRT Pulsar Survey (GCGPS) III: ORION - a multi-beam acceleration search pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27303) <a id="link39"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27303

**Authors:** Nishant Deo (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics, Indian Institute of Science Education and Research), Jyotirmoy Das (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Jayanta Roy (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Saurabh Raj (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Karel Adamek (Department of Physics, Silesian University in Opava, Opava, Czech Republic), Wesley Armour (Oxford e-Research Centre), Bhaswati Bhattacharyya (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Jayaram Chengalur (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Paulo C. C. Freire (Max-Planck-Institut fur Radioastronomie), Scott M. Ransom (National Radio Astronomy Observatory), Sridhar Gajendran (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Sanjay Kudale (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Mekhala V. Muley (National Centre for Radio Astrophysics), Adityan S (Loyola College, Nungambakkam, Chennai, India), Junhao Zhang (Department of Engineering Science, University of Oxford, Oxford, United Kingdom)

**Abstract:** Globular clusters host rich millisecond pulsar populations, but the known population remains incomplete. We present the observing strategy for Phase II of the Globular Clusters GMRT Pulsar Survey (GCGPS), which uses the SPOTLIGHT system to form 160 synthesised beams simultaneously, substantially expanding the instantaneous field of view compared with the two phased-array beams of Phase I. The configuration is tunable through the beam overlap and the number of contributing antennas, allowing spatial coverage to be balanced against sensitivity. We quantify the sensitivity losses from intra-channel dispersion smearing and finite sampling, which motivate the adopted dispersion-measure limits of 50 and 150 pc/cc for Bands 3 and 4. To process the resulting data, we introduce ORION, a GPU-accelerated pipeline that combines the high-throughput Fourier-domain filtering of PULSCAN with localised parameter refinement using PRESTO. In single-beam tests with simulated binary pulsars, ORION reduces processing time by a factor of approximately 15 to 30 relative to the CPU-based Phase I pipeline, with comparable or higher folded detection significance. On-sky validation with Cycle 49 observations recovered four known globular cluster pulsars with spin periods of approximately 5.4 to 406 ms. Using eight GPUs and 64 CPU cores, ORION processes full 160-beam observations in approximately 0.25 to 0.33 times the observation duration, corresponding to three to four times real-time throughput. Together, the Phase II strategy and ORION provide a scalable framework for expanding globular cluster pulsar searches, with future development targeting highly accelerated and jerked binary systems.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 40. [Tight asteroseismic upper limits on hot subdwarf magnetism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28443) <a id="link40"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28443

**Authors:** Nicholas Z. Rui, Linhao Ma

**Abstract:** The apparent absence of magnetism in subdwarf B stars (sdBs) has emerged as an outstanding observational puzzle, especially in light of their likely origin in binary interactions. Gravity-mode pulsations in some sdBs offer a unique opportunity to extend recent successes in asteroseismic magnetic field detection in red giants. In the presence of strong magnetic fields, gravity modes are expected to be significantly damped, suppressing their observed amplitudes. We place stringent upper limits on magnetic fields in 30 hot subdwarfs by using the observation of low-frequency dipole and quadrupole modes to rule out magnetic suppression. Even under conservative assumptions, our upper limits constrain near-surface magnetic fields to below a kilogauss in roughly half of our sample, with the strongest individual constraint reaching down to $\sim20\,\mathrm{G}$. These upper limits reinforce the apparent dearth of magnetic fields in hot subdwarfs and demonstrate asteroseismology as a powerful new probe of magnetism in these stars.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6

## 41. [PSR~B0355+54: An Unexpected Compendium of Nearly All Scintillation Arc Morphologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27005) <a id="link41"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27005

**Authors:** Zhen-Long Liao, Zhen Yan, Zhi-Qiang Shen, Rui Wang, Xun Shi, Ke-Jia Lee, Ju-Mei Yao, Xiao-Wei Wang, Zhi-Peng Huang, Kuo Liu, Yong-Hua Xu, Chu-Yuan Zhang, Fan Yang, Yang-Yang Lin

**Abstract:** Scintillation arcs are sensitive probes of multipath propagation, offering a window into the distribution of scattering material in the interstellar medium. Contrary to the common observational finding that individual pulsars exhibit only a limited range of scintillation arc morphologies, we report that PSR~B0355+54, observed simultaneously at 2.25/8.60~GHz with the Shanghai Tianma Radio Telescope, displays nearly the full range of known arc patterns, many of which evolve rapidly. Across 54 observations from 2020 to 2026, we detect scintillation arcs in 53/53 usable observations at 2.25~GHz and 33/49 at 8.60~GHz. At 2.25~GHz, the secondary spectra exhibit five broad morphological types: forward arcs, inverted arclets, diffuse arcs, diffuse power, and irregular structures, with prominent displaced arclets appearing in several observations. In contrast, the 8.60-GHz spectra are generally dominated by faint forward arcs. Despite these marked morphological differences, the scaled curvatures and their annual variations agree between the two frequencies, showing that both trace the same dominant scattering screen at a fractional distance of $s=0.069^{+0.015}_{-0.014}$ near the pulsar, further supporting a possible association with the \ion{H}{ii} region Sh~2-205. Simulations show that this diversity can arise from variations in the scattered-image distribution within this screen, while brightness reconstructions at 2.25~GHz resolve localized structure down to $\sim0.02~\mathrm{mas}$. Tracking four arclets over 10~h at 8.60~GHz, including two displaced arclets, reveals a common evolutionary trend consistent with the line of sight sweeping across small-scale scattering structure, helping explain the rapid arc evolution.

**Relevance:** 2
**Novelty:** 6
