## 90. [Floquet Instability of Photon-Axion Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26908) <a id="link90"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26908

**Authors:** Yuuki Sugiyama, Kazushige Ueda

**Abstract:** The discovery of axions would provide evidence for physics beyond the Standard Model. In this work, we investigate axion-photon conversion in the presence of a background magnetic field with simultaneous spatial and temporal periodicity. By performing a Lorentz transformation, the coupled axion-photon system is mapped onto a time-periodic linear system that can be analyzed using Floquet theorem. We find unstable modes associated with resonant crossing between Floquet replicas. The resulting complex Floquet exponents lead to exponentially growing modes, which we identify as a Floquet instability. Then, we determine the parameter regions in which this instability appears in a perturbative analysis. We further show that the Floquet instability enhances the axion-field amplitude generated from an initial photon field. Our results demonstrate that spatiotemporally periodic magnetic backgrounds provide a distinct mechanism for modifying axion-photon conversion through Floquet instability.

## 91. [Hidden engines in tidal disruption events: multimessenger signatures in a lepto-hadronic framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28182) <a id="link91"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28182

**Authors:** D. Kantzas, D. A. Langis, I. Liodakis, A. Paggi

**Abstract:** Tidal disruption events (TDEs) provide a unique laboratory for studying transient accretion, outflows, particle acceleration, and the possible connection between stellar disruptions and high-energy neutrinos. Motivated by recent candidate TDE-neutrino associations and by the forthcoming sensitivity of multimessenger facilities, we investigate under which physical conditions TDEs can be detected in MeV, GeV, and TeV gamma rays, and in high-energy neutrinos. We develop a lepto-hadronic emission framework and apply it to three representative sources: AT2019dsg, AT2019ehz, and ASASSN-14li. For each source we explore a set of physically motivated models that vary the proton and electron spectral indices, the minimum Lorentz factor of non-thermal protons, the particle energy partition, the acceleration efficiency, the magnetic compactness, and the presence of a compact corona. We compute the broadband spectral energy distributions, the associated muon-neutrino spectra, and compare them with reference sensitivities for COSI, Fermi/LAT, CTAO, and KM3NeT/ARCA. We find that hard proton spectra and/or large minimum proton Lorentz factors are the most favourable conditions for TeV gamma-ray and neutrino detectability, while GeV-bright models can be predominantly leptonic and neutrino quiet. AT2019dsg and AT2019ehz can reach the CTAO and KM3NeT benchmarks in hadronically favourable configurations, whereas ASASSN-14li is more naturally bright in the MeV-GeV band and constrained by its radio synchrotron emission. Our results highlight the importance of combining MeV, GeV, TeV, radio, and neutrino observations to distinguish between transparent, hidden, and lepton-dominated TDE emission regimes.

## 92. [Balmer breaks in accreting super-massive black hole binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27076) <a id="link92"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27076

**Authors:** Christopher Tiede, Zoltan Haiman

**Abstract:** We show that truncated circumbinary accretion disks around super-massive black hole binaries can produce Balmer breaks. For binaries interacting with surrounding disks in a truncated, non-accreting phase, gas accumulates at the cavity edge, and can create conditions conducive to generating spectral discontinuities from $n=2$ hydrogen bound-free absorption. We develop a simple model for predicting the presence of a Balmer edge and estimating the break strength, and we find that the strongest discontinuities are expected for binaries with periods of months to years and total masses $\gtrsim10^6\,\Msun$. These systems share considerable overlap with those binaries anticipated to appear in upcoming time-domain surveys---like LSST and the Roman HLTDS---as periodically variable quasars. We estimate that for typical feeding rates of a few tenths of their Eddington rate, a few-to-10\% of periodic binaries at $z\lesssim1$ should show prominent Balmer breaks, and that this fraction increases for the more massive black holes that can be discovered at higher redshift. We suggest that black hole binaries that truncate their accretion flows will appear as periodically variable sources, and will additionally be intrinsically X-ray weak, deficient in ionizing emission, and in favorable cases, produce notable Balmer edges in their spectra. This combination provides a complementary observational test for massive black hole binary candidates.

## 93. [Untangling Extreme Mass Ratio Inspirals and Massive Black Hole Binaries in LISA Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27642) <a id="link93"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27642

**Authors:** Rishav Agrawal, Clémence Allietta, Ollie Burke, Alvin J. K. Chua

**Abstract:** The Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA) will observe sources in the millihertz regime, such as Galactic Binaries (GBs), Massive Black Hole Binaries (MBHBs), and Extreme Mass Ratio Inspirals (EMRIs), so its data stream will be a superposition of an unknown number of gravitational-wave sources. We consider the joint inference of various representative combinations of MBHB and EMRI signals in LISA data (with assumed instrumental noise and stochastic GB foreground). Our Bayesian analyses confirm the expectation of weak inter-source coupling between EMRIs, and between EMRIs and MBHBs, despite strong intra-source parameter correlations. Weak EMRI-MBHB coupling enables a hierarchically sequential approach to EMRI and MBHB inference, rather than fully joint inference. We further show that imperfect MBHB subtraction, arising from missing modes or overlapping MBHBs, has only a weak impact on EMRI inference. For multiple MBHBs, we mitigate inference errors through a joint MBHB fit. We demonstrate this strategy on simulated LISA data containing noise, three MBHBs, and three EMRIs, jointly fitting and subtracting the MBHBs before sequentially inferring the EMRIs - ultimately observing little to no bias on all recovered astrophysical parameters. The notions described throughout our work are highly relevant to informing optimal strategies for the global-fit-based pipelines that are currently being developed for LISA.

## 94. [What Shapes the Ultraviolet Spectra of Type Ia Supernovae?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27807) <a id="link94"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27807

**Authors:** Snehasish Bhattacharjee, Yen-Chen Pan, Anirban Dutta, Shubham Gupta, Chia-Yu Cheng, Charles D. Kilpatrick, Matthew R. Siebert

**Abstract:** Ultraviolet (UV) observations of Type Ia supernovae (SNe Ia) exhibit significant diversity, but their physical origin remains poorly understood. We investigate UV spectra of SNe Ia observed with the Swift UltraViolet and Optical Telescope. Our expanded pre-maximum sample probes dependencies on decline rate, ejecta properties, and host environment. We recover the correlation between UV flux and decline rate near maximum in the near-UV (NUV), and find correlations at pre-maximum phases in both the NUV and mid-UV (MUV). This trend is strongly wavelength dependent, with slow decliners showing higher NUV but lower MUV flux than fast decliners. Synthetic TARDIS spectra varying the 56Ni abundance show that enhanced 56Ni suppresses the MUV while increasing the NUV, broadly reproducing the trend. We find no significant correlation between UV flux and ejecta velocity, while Si II 6355 pEW shows trends similar to those with decline rate. We also find no significant correlations with host-galaxy properties, although host environment may contribute to the remaining UV diversity among SNe with similar decline rates. Early-excess SNe show enhanced flux mainly in the NUV rather than a broad UV excess, suggesting that strong circumstellar interaction is not necessarily required to explain the observed excess emission. Peculiar subclasses show distinct UV properties, with brighter and hotter subclasses showing higher UV flux, although the current sample is small and does not cover all SN Ia subtypes. Our results suggest that the UV diversity of SNe Ia is shaped by multiple mechanisms that contribute differently across the UV wavelength range.

## 95. [Rapid Variability of Radiative Signatures from Driven Relativistic Reconnection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28034) <a id="link95"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28034

**Authors:** Clarissa Mora, Fabio Bacchini, Camille Granier, Daniel Groselj

**Abstract:** Rapid variability in energetic flares is a distinctive feature of high-energy emission from many magnetically dominated astrophysical sources. We investigate the radiative variability of driven relativistic magnetic reconnection using Particle-in-Cell simulations of strongly magnetized flux tubes pushed to merge at driving speed $v_{\rm push}$. This setup captures the onset of reconnection and provides a controlled model for single reconnection-driven flares near compact objects, where magnetic structures can be twisted, sheared, and forced to merge by turbulent accretion flows or relativistic frame dragging. We find that the merger efficiently accelerates particles into nonthermal distributions, producing synchrotron emission whose variability tracks the reconnection phases. We identify two regimes: a quasi-steady, weakly driven regime for $v_{push}\leq0.1\,c$, and strongly driven regime for $v_{\rm push}>0.1\,c$. The transition occurs when the driving exceeds the typical inflow-to-outflow velocity ratio of $\sim0.1$ in steady collisionless relativistic reconnection, i.e., the average rate at which reconnection can process incoming flux, potentially providing an additional source of variability. The lightcurves rise on $1$--$3$ light-crossing times of the reconnecting region, and the particle spectra develop power laws with index $p\simeq1.8$--$2.4$. These timescales are compatible with observed variability in high-energy sources, including the very-high-energy flares of M87$^*$ and the $\gamma$-ray flares of the Crab Nebula, making this model a viable framework for rapid flaring in active galactic nuclei and pulsar wind nebulae.

## 96. [Pulsar J1909+0749 and Supernova Remnant G42.1-0.2: A case of a pulsar that has exited the shell of its associated supernova remnant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28073) <a id="link96"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28073

**Authors:** R. Dokara, M. Kramer, A. Brunthaler, M. J. Keith, B. W. Stappers

**Abstract:** (Abridged) The ATNF pulsar catalogue features only 63 pulsars that are possibly associated with a supernova remnant (SNR). Even rarer is the scenario in which the pulsar has escaped the physical extent of its corresponding SNR. We aim to identify the serendipitously discovered shell-type object G42.1-0.2 as a SNR. The known pulsar (PSR) J1909+0749 lies well outside the shell of SNR G42.1-0.2, but it has a cometary tail pointing towards the centre of the SNR; we propose that the SNR and the pulsar originated from the same supernova explosion. By measuring the flux densities of the shell G42.1-0.2 from ${\sim}$200 MHz to ${\sim}$6 GHz, we establish the nonthermal nature of its emission. With the characteristic age of the pulsar, its distance from the Sun estimated from the dispersion measure (DM), and the angular separation between the pulsar and the centre of the shell, we infer the evolutionary state of the SNR and the transverse velocity of the pulsar. Using proper motion measurements, we compare the SNR-pulsar direction with the velocity of the pulsar. The radio spectral index of the shell is $-0.36 \pm 0.08$, confirming that the emission is primarily nonthermal synchrotron. The proper motion measurements are not well constrained, but the amplitude and the direction are consistent with the interpretation that the SNR and the pulsar are associated. If the characteristic age and the DM-based distance represent the true age and the true distance of the pulsar and the SNR, then the standard evolution equations suggest that the SNR must still be in the Sedov-Taylor phase, evolving in a medium with a density of about 1.5 cm$^{-3}$. In addition, as a result of the PSR-SNR association, the transverse velocity of the pulsar PSR J1909+0749 is estimated to be ${\sim}1460~\mathrm{km\,s}^{-1}$, ranking it among the highest transverse velocities observed for pulsars in the Milky Way.

## 97. [The T-matrix method for modeling the optical properties of grains in exoplanetary atmospheres](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28117) <a id="link97"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28117

**Authors:** Giambattista Aresu, Giovanni La Mura, Giacomo Mulas, Cesare Cecchi-Pestellini, Maria Antonia Iatì, Shadi Rezaei, Antonino Petralia, Daniele Locci, Giuseppina Micela, Franz Saija, Rosalba Saija

**Abstract:** Observations of exoplanetary atmospheres have revealed a rather complex co-existence between the gaseous and condensed phases, ranging from atmospheres apparently covered in clouds to clear ones, across a myriad of intermediate regimes. The interaction between solids and stellar radiation is of fundamental importance when forward or retrieval models are compared to observations. The simplest approach is to assume spherical particles, whose scattering and absorption properties are readily computed with Mie theory. More advanced computational methods, however, can also treat non-spherical condensates. Among these, the transition matrix approach has proven to provide a rigorous and efficient calculation of the extinction by non-spherical particles over a broad range of sizes and refractive indices. We here focus on the absorption and scattering cross sections of forsterite, spinel and silicon monoxide from the near to the far infrared range for particles of different sizes, with shapes ranging from compact ensembles of spheres to porous aggregates. We find that, depending on the size and the configuration chosen, the differences in extinction can be as high as a factor of 2-3, with structured grains being more efficient absorbers in the near infrared for sizes greater than 1 $\mu$m. In a fiducial transmission spectrum we show that in the $\lambda = 1-5\,\mu$m range the amount of spherical material needed to match the contribution of the structured grains can be a factor 2 higher. Such calculations, available for the community through a Zenodo repository, prove to be useful for interpreting recent and future observations of exoplanetary atmospheres.

## 98. [The SIMPLIFI Survey: Polarization of Dense Filaments with SOFIA III. Relative orientations of antenna temperature gradients with the magnetic field in the DR21 molecular cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27090) <a id="link98"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27090

**Authors:** Mark Heyer, Thushara G. S. Pillai, Jens Kauffmann, Juan D. Soler, Daniel Seifried, Laura M. Fissel, Gabriel A. P. Franco, Paul F. Goldsmith, Philip C. Myers, Helmut Wiesemeyer, Karl M. Menten

**Abstract:** We investigate magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence in the DR21 molecular cloud using far infrared polarimetry from SOFIA HAWC+ and molecular spectroscopy from the Nobeyama 45~m telescope. The Velocity Channel Gradient (VChG) method is applied to thin velocity slices of the 12CO and 13CO J=1-0 emission to determine the antenna temperature gradients and their orientations in the plane of the sky. The relative orientations of the gradients with respect to the magnetic field position angles enable an evaluation of theoretically predicted alignment of elongated, turbulent eddies for Alfvenic, fast and slow modes and self-gravity. The projected Rayleigh statistic is calculated for both the full cloud and 6 regions of interest to provide a quantitative assessment of these alignments. Our analysis finds mostly parallel alignment of the gradient direction and the local magnetic field orientation in the Main Ridge of DR21 that comprises most of the cloud mass. While parallel alignment can result from fast MHD modes or cloud collapse, previous studies show spectroscopic evidence for the large scale collapse of the DR21 cloud, which supports self-gravity as the primary cause of the observed parallel alignment. One of the six sub-filaments that are connected to the Main Ridge exhibits mostly perpendicular alignment of the position angles of the gradient and magnetic field. These perpendicular relative orientations may be a product of Alfven and slow modes in a magnetized, sub-Alfvenic medium even if the sub-filament is accreting onto the Main Ridge.

## 99. [Cosmic-ray melt windows in interstellar ice make a four-carbon ketose but not the sugar pattern of G+0.693-0.027](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27778) <a id="link99"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27778

**Authors:** S. Saito, M. Yajima, T. Kenmotsu, M. Wada, K. Kobayashi, H. Nakamura, G. Chiaki

**Abstract:** Erythrulose (ERU), a four-carbon (C4) ketose, is reported towards the Galactic-centre cloud G+0.693-0.027 at a column density comparable to that of glycolaldehyde (GA), while the three-carbon (C3) sugars are not detected. We test whether melt windows opened in interstellar ice by cosmic-ray heavy ions can reproduce this pattern, with each rate fixed to a published value where available. A track model for eleven cosmic-ray nuclei determines the rate and duration of the liquid windows and the energy deposited inside and outside the melts. A radical network including C1 radicals from formaldehyde and methanol follows the inventory through repeated events. Radiolysis between events uses measured half-life doses of organics in cold ice. Hydrated GA opens a channel that preferentially forms the C4 ketose, so the melt-window chemistry can account for the ketose identity of ERU, although cold surface chemistry also yields it. The overall pattern, however, is not reproduced. The C1 radicals add one carbon at a time: dihydroxyacetone is at 15 times its limit when ERU/GA reaches the observed value. The ions deposit 0.41 eV per molecule per event outside the melts, which destroys the sugars between events; the ratio is reached only for half-life doses above 1000 eV, against measured values below 5 eV. The exposure is less certain: 2940 whole-grain melting events, 65 Myr at an ionisation rate $\zeta=10^{-14}$ s$^{-1}$. Within 7 Myr it needs $\zeta\gtrsim9.3\times10^{-14}$ s$^{-1}$, 9.3 times the dense-gas limit in the Central Molecular Zone, or $5.8\times10^{-15}$ s$^{-1}$ if the trapped radicals recombine. With standard assumptions the sugars of G+0.693 are not made in cosmic-ray melt windows. The pattern would need C1 radicals that do not add to the sugar radicals, sugars that survive the solid-phase dose and a local source of MeV ions with $\zeta\gtrsim2\times10^{-13}$ s$^{-1}$.

## 100. [Constraining Solid HNCO and Its Environment in Open JWST Data with Laboratory Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27949) <a id="link100"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27949

**Authors:** Ruslan Nakibov (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia), Varvara Karteyeva (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia), Katerina Borshcheva (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia, Institute of Astronomy of the Russian Academy of Sciences, Moscow, Russia), Igor Petrashkevich (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia), Mikhail Medvedev (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia), Anton Vasyunin (Research Laboratory for Astrochemistry, Ural Federal University, Yekaterinburg, Russia)

**Abstract:** The presence of HNCO in the gaseous phase is well known for the interstellar medium, but the molecule is not detected yet in the solid phase. We report the first laboratory-confirmed identification of solid HNCO at 2260 cm$^{-1}$ in open JWST data using laboratory infrared spectra. For that, we grew binary mixtures of HNCO with main constituents of interstellar ice using ISEAge setup at 10 K: HNCO:H$_2$O = 1:10, HNCO:CO = 1:10, HNCO:CO$_2$ = 1:10, HNCO:CH$_3$OH~=~1:10. We present transmission infrared spectra of pure HNCO and HNCO-bearing ices along with the corresponding band strengths at 10 K. The fitting of open JWST observational data with the laboratory reference spectra in a 10--23 K temperature range show that solid HNCO is predominantly located in the apolar layer rich in CO. The abundance of solid HNCO is approximately estimated as 0.04--0.15\% relative to solid H$_2$O. The positive correlation between HNCO and N$_2$O suggests their co-formation via the chemical route that includes solid N$_2$. The results are also supported by the numerical modeling of HNCO formation in protostellar objects with predicted abundance of 0.06--0.11\%. We calculated the upper limits for CH$_3$CN toward two protostars and evaluated its abundance to solid water using the spectroscopic fitting and modeling.

Sengupta (CNRS, CEA, AIM, Universite Paris-Saclay, Universite Paris Cite, 91191, Gif-sur-Yvette, France), R. Silver (NASA Goddard Space Flight Center, Greenbelt, MD 20771, USA, School of Physics and Astronomy, University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN 55455, USA), X. Zhao (Cahill Center for Astrophysics, California Institute of Technology, 1216 East California Boulevard, Pasadena, CA 91125, USA)

**Abstract:** We present a broadband, multiwavelength study of the interplay between the soft X-ray excess (SE), ultra-fast outflows (UFOs), and warm absorbers (WAs) in 21 active galactic nuclei (AGN; 0.1 <= z  2$, consistent with previous studies. However, 9/21 AGN require the hybrid model, suggesting that the SE has multiple origins whose relative contributions vary. This is especially evident in UFO hosts, where 4/7 observations favor the hybrid model. Compared with sources without UFOs, UFO hosts show lower WC temperatures, harder hot-corona spectra, and positive hardness ratios. These trends should be treated cautiously because of the small sample and their dependence on spectral parameters and instrumental response. We find no significant differences between AGN with and without WAs. Our correlation analysis finds links between the amplification factor and WC temperature, WC optical depth and hardness ratio, and WC slope and optical depth with UV luminosity. We also find a tentative anticorrelation between WC optical depth and UFO outflow velocity.

## 102. [Elementary-Symmetric Ricci Gravity: Metric Formulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26980) <a id="link102"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26980

**Authors:** Xingpao Suo, Dianxu Ma, Yuqiong Shan, Xingyan Suo

**Abstract:** We investigate a four-dimensional metric theory of gravity whose Lagrangian is a linear combination of the elementary symmetric polynomials of the Ricci tensor. We derive the field equations and characterize Einstein vacua and a real, diagonalizable class of parallel-Ricci vacua. The latter includes direct products of two constant-curvature surfaces and can exhibit a hierarchy of curvature radii. The Einstein vacua possess multiple branches associated with the distinct roots of a polynomial, whose multiple roots define boundaries in parameter space. We derive the linearized equations on maximally symmetric backgrounds, distinguish the scalar and spin-2 degrees of freedom, and then analyze their classical linear stability. We also obtain the generalized Friedmann constraint and the associated homogeneous evolution equations. An illustrative spatially flat solution evolves from the neighborhood of an unstable de Sitter solution toward a stable de Sitter solution. This behavior may motivate further investigation into early- and late-time accelerated expansion and provide a gravity-driven mechanism for automatic end of inflation.

## 103. [Probing the Optical/UV Continuum Variability of Active Galactic Nuclei with the Damped Random Walk Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27806) <a id="link103"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27806

**Authors:** Xiang-Wei Ning (NNU), Wen-Jie Hu (NNU), Zi-Han Yuan (NNU), Yi Tang (NNU), Yan-Song Ma (NNU), Yu-Meng Gua (NNU), Le-Yi Huang (NNU), Wei-Hao Bian (NNU)

**Abstract:** Optical/UV continuum variability over baselines of up to 20 yr is investigated using the damped random walk (DRW) model for 1214 active galactic nuclei (AGNs) compiled from Y. Shen et al. and C. J. Burke et al. Among these, 115 AGNs with reliable DRW fitting and unbiased DRW parameters (timescale $\tau_{2500}$ and amplitude $\sigma_{2500}$ at the rest-frame 2500~\AA) are used to examine the relations between the variability and the accretion properties. We found a significant correlation of $\tau_{2500}$ with black hole mass ($M_{\rm BH}$) and dimensionless accretion rate ($\dot{\mathscr{M}}$) of the form $\tau_{2500} \propto M_{\rm BH}^{0.50\pm0.07} \dot{\mathscr{M}}^{0.27\pm0.04}$, while the dependence on the continuum luminosity at 5100~\AA\ and redshift becomes insignificant after controlling for $M_{\rm BH}$. $\tau_{2500}$ also correlate with the mass accretion rate ($\dot{M}$) as $\tau_{2500} \propto \dot{M}^{0.41\pm0.04}$, which is consistent with the thermal timescale expected from the standard thin-disk model within $3\sigma$ uncertainties; while the average value is consistent with the expectations of the model, the scatter in the relation can be due to differences in the viscosity parameter ($\alpha$) or in disk temperature profiles. Assuming that \tauuv\ corresponds to the accretion disk thermal timescale, the disk-emitting radius at rest-frame 2500~\AA\ ($R_{2500}$) is found to correlate with $M_{\rm BH}$ as $R_{2500} \propto M_{\rm BH}^{0.53\pm0.05}$. Using the microlensing-based $R_{2500}-M_{\rm BH}$ relation of C. W. Morgan et al., $\alpha$ is statistically estimated to be $0.04\pm0.01$.

## 104. [Estimator Precision and Selection for Up-the-Ramp Sampling with a High-Read-Noise InGaAs Camera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27742) <a id="link104"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27742

**Authors:** Wei-Jie Meng, Fang-Yu Xu, Zhen-Yu Jin, Guang-Qian Liu

**Abstract:** The noise properties of an InGaAs camera vary with gain, requiring a trade-off between implementation complexity and precision. Using generalized least squares (GLS) with known covariance as the precision benchmark, we examine the applicability and precision penalties of ordinary least squares (OLS) and correlated double sampling (CDS) at different noise operating points. We define $\eta=fT/\sigma_\mathrm{R}^2$, where $f$ is the total pixel count rate, $T$ the fitting span, and $\sigma_\mathrm{R}$ the single-read noise. For constant flux, uniform sampling, and independent white read noise plus Poisson noise, established finite-sampling variance expressions give equal OLS and CDS variances at $\eta=10$ for $N\ge4$ reads; OLS is favored below this boundary and CDS above it. Analytical and Monte Carlo comparisons for three gain modes quantify the precision loss of the better simplified estimator relative to GLS. In high-gain on-sky data, choosing OLS at 20 s and CDS at 90 s increases the background residual standard deviation by 3.55% and 3.01%, respectively, relative to two-iteration feasible GLS (FGLS). Photometry of 21 common sources does not clearly resolve most estimator differences. The observed OLS-CDS crossover occurs earlier than the ideal prediction, indicating that temporal covariance beyond the ideal model also affects the selection boundary. Observational validation is limited to high gain; medium- and low-gain choices rely on theory and simulations using calibrated parameters. OLS or CDS can replace GLS with a small precision penalty at suitable operating points. The ideal criterion provides an initial selection rule, whose practical boundary requires validation against observed temporal covariance.

## 105. [A bright X-ray flaring event from the intermediate supergiant fast X-ray transient IGR J00370+6122 observed with NuSTAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27925) <a id="link105"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27925

**Authors:** L. Ducci, M. M. Serim, P. Romano, E. Bozzo, L. Ji, C. Malacaria

**Abstract:** We present an analysis of a long NuSTAR observation of the intermediate supergiant fast X-ray transient (SFXT) IGR J00370+6122, obtained around the peak of its orbital modulation. The observation has a net exposure ~93 ks. During the observation, the source displayed a bright flaring event that was resolved into numerous short flares with durations of 10-1000 s. The 3-70 keV luminosity spanned a dynamic range of ~200, from ~1E34 to ~1.8E36 erg/s; the maximum was reached for only ~40 s in total. The spectrum is well described by an absorbed double-Comptonisation model, with parameters consistent with those found in other X-ray pulsars accreting at similarly low luminosities. This is the first detection of a double-hump continuum in IGR J00370+6122, which extends this spectral state to the class of intermediate SFXTs. We searched for the previously reported ~346 s and ~674 s periodicities and find no significant signal at either period, which makes their interpretation as the neutron star spin period less likely. Using ~21 yr of Swift/BAT monitoring, we refine the orbital period of the system to P_orb=15.6654 +/- 0.0008 d. Finally, we discuss the observed flaring activity in the framework of quasi-spherical settling accretion, which is expected at the observed luminosities.

## 106. [On the Stability of Tolman Oppenheimer Volkoff Fireholes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28140) <a id="link106"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28140

**Authors:** Pablo Laguna, Octavio Guerrero, Kostas Kokkotas

**Abstract:** We investigate the radial stability of singular Tolman--Oppenheimer--Volkoff fireholes with a radiation equation of state. These configurations contain a naked, negative point-mass singularity surrounded by a positive-density fluid, whose mass profile develops a Schwarzschild-like plateau before approaching an extended, self-similar atmosphere. Using the Pr\"ufer phase method to identify the fundamental radial mode, we consider two outer boundary conditions: vanishing Lagrangian pressure perturbation (open firehole) and vanishing radial displacement (boxed firehole). For both, the fundamental squared frequency changes from positive to negative as the outer boundary exceeds a critical radius $R_c$. We find that $R_c$ follows approximately the same scaling with the central mass parameter as the equilibrium firehole radius $R$. However, the stability thresholds differ significantly: $R_c^{\rm O}<R$ for the open firehole, whereas $R_c^{\rm B}\simeq R$ for the boxed one. The regular zero mode is the derivative of the equilibrium family with respect to the inner lapse normalization at fixed central mass. This gives the boxed threshold a turning-point interpretation and, together with a scaling symmetry of the TOV equations, implies $R_c^{\rm B}\to R$ in the limit of large central mass. Variational bounds show that the rigid wall is the most stabilizing separated, self-adjoint outer boundary condition and that the unbounded configuration is unstable. Tolman--Oppenheimer--Volkoff fireholes are therefore radially unstable unless confined within approximately their firehole radius.

**Relevance:** 1

## 107. [AI and The Enclosure of the Scientific Commons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27251) <a id="link107"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27251

**Authors:** P. K. G. Williams (Center for Astrophysics | Harvard,Smithsonian)

**Abstract:** The scientific literature can be viewed as an "information commons:" an intangible resource that "no one owns and is shared by everyone." There are well-established economic and political frameworks for understanding such commons, emerging from the history of agricultural common lands found in places like preindustrial England. This article provides a primer on the history and these frameworks, and considers a key difference between the information commons and the fields of yore: while information commons are robust and immensely valuable --- some can be said to rank among humanity's greatest achievements --- the English agricultural commons no longer meaningfully exist. The latter are gone not because of the well-known "tragedy of the commons," but rather a phenomenon known as the enclosure movement (~1600--1800) that transformed the common lands into private property. Many observers, then and now, judge enclosure to have been theft at a societal scale. This article argues that artificial intelligence (AI) provides precisely the tools needed to enable a previously-infeasible enclosure of the information commons. Although AI-enabled enclosure does not operate identically to physical enclosure, it shares the key features of turning the commons into private wealth and undermining the social systems that support them (via "slop spam"). The concepts presented in this article can provide a scholarly basis for interrogating the foreboding feeling that AI may represent not just a smash-and-grab, but an attack upon a whole way of life.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 4

## 108. [The GLEAM 4-Jy (G4Jy) Sample: V. Broadband spectral analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27171) <a id="link108"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27171

**Authors:** Sarah V. White, Siegfried A. Gawenda, Giulia Macario, Samuel P. Hansen, Ross J. Turner, Thomas M. O. Franzen, Joseph R. Callingham, Randall Wayth, Kshitij Thorat

**Abstract:** The G4Jy Sample consists of 1,863 of the brightest sources in the southern sky, as selected at 151 MHz using the GaLactic and Extragalactic All-sky MWA (GLEAM) survey. We supplement existing, low-frequency radio data by completing visual inspection of radio images from the Rapid ASKAP Continuum Survey (RACS) at 887.5, 1367.5 and 1655.5 MHz. Doing so on a source-by-source basis allows us to assess the quality of the association of the radio emission, which we indicate via a flag system. Having multiple radio flux-densities allows us to construct broadband radio spectral energy distributions (SEDs), and these extend up to 20 GHz for sources that also have data from the Australia Telescope Compact Array (ATCA). We fit polynomial functions to these SEDs, and determine the distribution of sources that are best-fit by absorption models, these accounting for 11 per cent of the sample. The SEDs are further modelled using the Radio AGN in Semi-analytic Environments (RAiSE) framework, for three assumed density-profiles (set by typical halo masses of 10^12.5, 10^13.5, and 10^14.5 Msol). The derived source ages show the expected distribution in the radio-power--size (P--D) diagram, whilst derived jet powers show non-linear behaviour. The latter is believed to be the result of higher densities, associated with higher magnetic fields, restricting the expansion of the radio jets/lobes.

## 109. [Large-scale Vortices in the Solar Photosphere: Statistical Properties and the Reliability of Local Correlation Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27222) <a id="link109"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27222

**Authors:** Mashael Aldhafeeri, Suzana S. A. Silva, Istvan Ballai, Gary Verth, Haruhisa Iijima, Hideyuki Hotta, Viktor Fedun

**Abstract:** Vortices are observed throughout the solar atmosphere, spanning a wide range of spatial scales. Although small-scale vortices have been studied extensively, mesoscale vortices remain less explored, and it is still unclear whether they can be reliably recovered from observations. Mesogranular-scale motions are recovered from the R2D2 simulations by applying Gaussian spatial smoothing to the horizontal velocity field with full width at half maximum (FWHM) values of 2.5-4.0 Mm. To evaluate the accuracy of local correlation tracking (LCT) and its suitability for identifying mesogranular vortices, we apply LCT to synthetic intensity maps at $\tau = 1$ using the same spatial windows and temporal averaging windows of 10-60 minutes. Vortices are identified in both the reference and LCT-derived velocity fields using the $\Gamma$ method. For all tested parameter combinations, LCT underestimates the number of mesogranular-scale vortices. The best overall recovery is obtained for relatively small spatial smoothing scales (FWHM $\leq 3.5$ Mm) and short averaging windows ($\leq 20$ minutes), which provide the most reliable vorticity and lifetime statistics while maintaining strong agreement in vortex radius and area distributions. Our analysis shows that only a modest fraction of LCT-detected vortices correspond to vortices identified in the reference velocity field (3.54%-17.88%), depending on FWHM and temporal averaging. This indicates that vortex-driven dynamics inferred from LCT-detected vortices should be interpreted with caution. Our results suggest that applying the $\Gamma$ method to LCT-derived velocity fields can recover useful statistical properties of mesoscale vortical structures, especially their temporal trends and characteristic sizes.

## 110. [Hybrid Stars with Different Amount of Quark Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28408) <a id="link110"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28408

**Authors:** Milena Albino, Tuhin Malik, Márcio Ferreira, Constança Providência

**Abstract:** In a previous work, we explored the possibility of neutron stars with large quark cores. Through Bayesian inference, we obtained large sets of equations of state imposing constraints regarding the properties of nuclear matter, mass-radius data from NICER, the GW170817 data from Virgo-LIGO collaboration, perturbative QCD and causality. We also imposed that the maximum mass neutron star (NS$\text{max}$) must have a large quark core. In this work, we obtain a new set of equations of state (SmallQ set) with the same constraints as before. However, we relaxed the last constraint, imposing only that NS$\text{max}$ must have quark core, regardless of its size. We compare the results with the set from our previous paper constrained by NICER and GW170817 observations NJL-GW. For both sets, we use the Relativistic Mean Field model with nonlinear meson interactions for the hadron phase and the Nambu--Jona-Lasinio model with four- and eight-quark interaction terms for the quark phase. The SmallQ set has higher values of phase transition density, with 3 -- 4.5 $\rho_0$, compared to the NJL-GW, with 2 -- 2.5 $\rho_0$. The new set describes NSs with smaller radius and smaller tidal deformability, and is more compatible with NICER and GW170817 data. A difference in the quark matter composition is observed, with a larger quark strange fraction occurring in the SmallQ set. Within this set, $2 M_\odot$ NSs may contain little to no quark matter. It is shown that there is a non negligible fraction of EOS whose mass-radius curve presents a positive slope for 1.8 $M_\odot$ stars, while for nucleonic stars this fraction is negligible and for hyperonic stars the slope is always negative.

## 111. [Stability Analysis of Axion Dark Energy Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27514) <a id="link111"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27514

**Authors:** Ankan Mukherjee, Nandan Roy

**Abstract:** We investigate the stability of an axion dark energy model characterized by a periodic scalar-field potential motivated by pseudo Nambu Goldstone bosons. While such models are well suited to drive late-time cosmic acceleration and can exhibit rich dynamical features, including oscillatory behavior of the dark-energy equation-of-state parameter, their viability crucially depends on the existence of stable cosmological solutions. By reformulating the background evolution equations as an autonomous dynamical system, we identify the fixed points corresponding to radiation-dominated, matter-dominated, and dark energy dominated epochs. Using both analytical and numerical techniques, we analyze the stability properties of these fixed points within the observationally allowed region of parameter space. Our results show that the radiation- and matter-dominated phases emerge naturally as saddle points, ensuring a standard cosmological evolution, while the late-time dark energy dominated solution acts as a stable attractor. This guarantees the robustness of cosmic acceleration against variations in initial conditions. Overall, our study establishes the axion dark energy model as a dynamically consistent and observationally viable candidate for explaining the late-time accelerated expansion of the Universe.

## 112. [Deriving Interplanetary Sector Boundary Crossings using Potential Field Source Surface Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28123) <a id="link112"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28123

**Authors:** Stephan G. Heinemann, Manuela Temmer, Jens Pomoell, Mathew Owens, Emilia Kilpua, Matti ala-Lahti, Andreas Wagner

**Abstract:** The Heliospheric Current Sheet (HCS) and associated interplanetary sector boundaries fundamentally structure the solar wind environment, modulating coronal mass ejection (CME) evolution and solar energetic particle transport. In this study, we evaluate the capability of a static Potential Field Source Surface (PFSS; $R_{ss}=2.5R_\odot$) coronal model to directly map interplanetary sector boundary crossings from the Sun to 1 au without dynamic heliospheric propagation models. We compare ballistically transported HCS crossings derived from GONG-ADAPT and SDO/HMI magnetic maps against sector boundary crossing observations from the SBCross catalog. PFSS modeling reproduces the large-scale interplanetary magnetic field topology well ($J\approx0.50$), achieving hit rates (POD) of 0.64 for GONG-ADAPT and 0.76 for HMI. Carrington synoptic maps outperform flux-transport modeled maps, suggesting that the short-term observational noise from data assimilation degrades topological matching more than map "aging". Predictive skill shows a strong solar cycle dependence, performing significantly better during solar maxima (POD=0.70-0.81) than solar minima (POD=0.56-0.67), attributed to more distinct crossings due to higher HCS inclination angles during maximum. Matched events exhibit travel times close to ballistic estimates of 4.8-4.9 days with an average timing difference centered near zero ($\pm1.1$ days) and 90% of events within $\pm 1.8$ days. The hit rate of HCS structures that interact with CMEs does not degrade (POD = 0.67-0.83), despite propagating $\approx7$ hours faster on average, implying that the CME interaction causes only minor structural changes to the HCS. Larger timing differences (>24 hours) are associated with flatter current sheets. Overall, static PFSS extrapolations offer a computationally inexpensive, effective approach for mapping global sector boundary structure.

## 114. [The Effect of Field Line Tracing Height on Wang-Sheeley-Arge Solar Wind Predictions at Earth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28274) <a id="link114"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.28274

**Authors:** Satabdwa Majumdar, Martin A. Reiss, Pete Riley, Charles N. Arge

**Abstract:** (Abridged abstract): A key component of the widely used Wang-Sheeley-Arge model is the tracing of magnetic field lines in the solar corona, as that largely determines the modeled open/closed field regions and in turn the predicted large-scale solar wind structure. We demonstrate that by slightly raising the height from which magnetic field lines are traced from the photosphere (R$_{su}$), we can improve the prediction of solar wind high-speed enhancements (HSEs). We find a higher R$_{su}$ produces more accurate estimates of the HSE start time, duration, and peak speed, and enables the detection of HSEs that were missed with the standard R$_{su}$. These improvements are primarily associated with changes in the great-circle angular distance, while the flux tube expansion factors remain largely unchanged. A higher R$_{su}$ produces smoother open-field boundaries and reduces small-scale fragmentation within open-field regions. These morphological changes lead to more stable magnetic footpoint mappings and consequently more robust GCD calculations. To assess the statistical relevance of these findings, we extend the analysis to 53 Carrington rotations and perform a multi-metric evaluation of model performance. The results indicate that higher R$_{su}$ improves HSE predictions while reducing false alarms. We thus find that the choice of R$_{su}$ represents an important and previously underexplored parameter that can help enhance our ambient solar wind modeling and forecasting capabilities.

**Abstract:** It was already recognised in the 2004 and 2014 SKA Science Case Chapters: "The Exploration of the Unknown" and "The SKA and the Unknown Unknowns", respectively, that the exploration of the unknown is an extremely important concept in realising the potential of any new instrument. This is especially true in an era of limited budgets but expanding analysis algorithms and capabilities. While this Chapter considers transients and environments, the ethos of these previous Chapters should be considered and embraced broadly and can be applied more broadly across SKAO science. We do not repeat in detail the important background these Chapters provide but encourage the reader to review them before reading this Chapter. In the last decade of radio transient astronomy, we have seen the value of these approaches with the expansion of the FRB population, the discovery of long period pulsars and transients and radio emission from white dwarfs to name just a few. With the SKAO, we should push the explored parameter space further and in new directions in all of the sensitivity, field-of-view, time, spectral, spectro-dynamic, polarisation and imaging domains. We should utilise approaches such as machine learning in allowing us to address the complexity that arises from the expanded parameter space and increased data rates and candidate sources. We will briefly summarise some of the impressive progress that the SKA pathfinders and precursors have made in these areas and what these mean for the SKA telescopes. We will also discuss ways in which it might be possible to push the current capabilities.

## 117. [Analytical Chemistry at the Planetary Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27197) <a id="link117"></a>

**ArXiv ID:** 2610.27197

**Authors:** Mavia Anjum

**Abstract:** Planetary science depends on chemical measurements made under conditions very different from those in a terrestrial laboratory. This review treats the analytical methods of planetary science as one connected chain, from orbital remote sensing through in-situ measurements by landers and rovers and sample collection and curation, to the micro-analysis of returned material on Earth. Orbital methods, including visible and near-infrared reflectance, thermal emission and gamma-ray and neutron spectroscopy, are discussed first. In-situ techniques are then reviewed, including laser-induced breakdown spectroscopy (LIBS), micro-focus X-ray fluorescence (XRF), deep-ultraviolet Raman and fluorescence spectroscopy, evolved gas analysis with gas chromatography-mass spectrometry (GC-MS) and miniaturized mass spectrometry. Contamination control and curation practices are described, followed by laboratory techniques such as secondary ion mass spectrometry (SIMS and NanoSIMS), atom probe tomography (APT), thermal ionization mass spectrometry (TIMS), synchrotron micro-XRF and X-ray absorption near-edge structure (XANES) spectroscopy. Accuracy, calibration and reference materials are discussed as the link between in-situ and laboratory data, and the role of chemometrics and data fusion is examined. Flight and laboratory instruments are compared in terms of spatial resolution, detection limit, sample consumption and throughput. Active neutron-gamma spectroscopy planned for the Dragonfly mission to Titan is also discussed. This comparison shows that in-situ and laboratory techniques are complementary. In-situ instruments provide context and select samples, while laboratory techniques provide isotopic, molecular and speciation data that cannot yet be obtained in situ.

**Relevance:** 1
**Novelty:** 3

# Paper Selection Prompt

## System Prompt

> You are a helpful paper reading assistant whose job is to read daily posts from ArXiv and rank the papers in order according to your friend's interests.
> Your job is to carefully read the paper titles and abstracts below and determine how well they match the criteria below.

## User Prompt

> ## Instructions
> 
> Write the response in JSONL format with {ARXIVID, COMMENT, RELEVANCE, NOVELTY} on each line, one for each paper.
> 
> - ARXIVID: should be the ArXiv ID.
> - COMMENT: for papers with RELEVANCE 7-10, briefly identify the specific matching criterion in at most 10 words. For papers with RELEVANCE 1-6, use an empty string. Never explain why a paper is irrelevant.
> - RELEVANCE: should be a score from 1-10.
> - NOVELTY: should be a score from 1-10.
> 
> 
> ## Scoring Criteria
> 
> > The "Relevance" score measures how closely the paper aligns with the core topics of the prompt.
> > The "Novelty" score assesses the originality and impact of the paper.
> > They are two **ORTHONORMAL** axes and **SHOULD NOT** be confused with each other.
> > When determining the final ranking of papers, the relevance score should be given twice as much weight as the novelty score.
> 
> ### Relevance Scoring
> 
> - Relevance 9-10 (Completely Relevant)
>   - Focus: Fully aligned with core topics with no deviation, score the highest if contains relevant keywords in it.
>   - Examples: Papers focused on foundational methods or theoretical research whose topic directly relates to core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 7-8 (Relevant)
>   - Focus: Retain a solid link to the main research area, though may touch on peripheral elements.
>   - Examples: Papers whose topics generally align with the core topics, but may other aspects or applications not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Focus: Maintains a link to the core topics but also extends into at least one other domain/area beyond the primary focus.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results have implications for the core topics but are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 3-4 (Irrelevant)
>   - Focus: Largely outside our interests with only very weak association to our topics.
>   - Examples: Papers whose results may have limited impact on the core topics and are not directly related to the core topics.
> 
> - Relevance 1-2 (Ignore)
>   - Focus: Purely unrelated to our topics. Completely a different domain.
> 
> ### Novelty Scoring
> 
> - Novelty 9-10 (Breakthrough)
>   - Definition: Groundbreaking methods/theory/application introducing new directions or solving major challenges.
>   - Examples: Entirely new paradigm for core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 7-8 (Improvements)
>   - Definition: Substantial insights/enhancements, though not a full paradigm shift.
>   - Examples: Modifications on existing methods yielding significantly better results. Applications to new datasets that directly address the core topics. Re-analysis of old datasets using new methods that directly address the core topics.
> 
> - Novelty 5-6 (Borderline)
>   - Definition: Incremental contributions with possible long-term benefits, not immediately transformative.
> 
> - Novelty 3-4 (Tangential)
>   - Definition: Minor or domain-specific improvements with limited broader impact.
> 
> - Novelty 1-2 (Low)
>   - Definition: Methods/application not applicable to core topics.
>

> ## Relevant Topics
> 
> Use the following relevance criteria when considering which papers to keep. To be rated as highly relevant, a paper must fit at least one of the relevant topics below. Papers which fit multiple categories should be rated more highly.
> 
> 1. Near-Field Cosmology
>    - Relevant: Astrophysics work focusing on dwarf satellite galaxies of the Milky Way, extragalactic satellite luminosity functions, measurements of dark matter density profiles (core-cusp), abundances of dwarf galaxies, signs of historic interactions between the Milky Way and other galaxies (e.g., the accreted stellar halo). Both theoretical and observational work is relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on Milky Way properties like the stellar disk, bar, and Galactic center. Papers that focus on higher mass galaxies, like massive spirals and elliptical galaxies.
> 
> 2. Resolved Star Formation Histories
>    - Relevant: Observational work deriving star formation histories from observed color-magnitude diagrams of galaxies. Theoretical work measuring star formation histories of simulated galaxies is also relevant, but should be weighted lower.
> 
> 3. Star Formation Quenching
>    - Relevant: Observational work discussing the quenching of star formation in galaxies, **particularly dwarf galaxies**. Work on higher mass galaxies may still be relevant, but should be weighted lower. Theoretical work on the quenching of star formation of dwarf galaxies is also relevant.
> 
> 4. Stellar Models
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new stellar models, either stellar evolutionary tracks (i.e., stellar interior models) or stellar atmosphere models. Work presenting new tables of bolometric corrections is also relevant.
> 
> 5. Numerical Galaxy Simulations
>    - Relevant: Theoretical work presenting new numerical simulations of galaxies is relevant, especially high-resolution simulations. Simulations that include new physics models (e.g., ionizing radiation, cosmic ray transport) should be given increased weight. Work analyzing previously published simulations should be weighted lower, unless the work also pertains to one of the other relevant topics. Semi-analytic work on galaxies that matches the other criteria is also relevant.
>    - Irrelevant: Numerical simulations that do not focus on galaxies and do not fulfill other criteria are not relevant. Examples include simulations of tidal disruption events, black hole accretion disks, and planet formation.
> 
> 6. Star Formation Driven Outflows
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the role the star formation processes play on driving galaxy-scale gas outflows. Measurements of mass-loading factors are particularly relevant. Work commenting on the phase structure of the outflows is also relevant. Work on galaxies more massive than the LMC should be weighted lower.
>    - Irrelevant: Papers that focus on outflows driven by sources other than stellar feedback, such as AGN-driven feedback. Papers are relevant if they include discussion of both star-formation-driven outflows and other types of outflows.
> 
> 7. Circumgalactic Medium
>    - Relevant: Observational and theoretical work examining the circumgalactic media (CGM) of dwarf galaxies is relevant. Increased weight should be given to work that connects the CGM to ISM outflows from the galaxy.
> 
> 
> 
> **Keywords:**
> 
> - Relevant: Near-field cosmology, satellite luminosity function, core-cusp, substructure, resolved star formation history, satellite quenching, mass-loading factor
> - Irrelevant: bar, Galactic center, tidal disruption event (TDE), supermassive black hole (SMBH)
